- `aHhB3sjGjkI` | [Agents Building Agents - Alfonso Graziano, Nearform](../sources/20260628_aHhB3sjGjkI.md) | 2026-06-28 | transcript: `raw/20260628_aHhB3sjGjkI/aHhB3sjGjkI.en-orig.vtt` | Alfonso Graziano (tech lead at Nearform, a services company; O'Reilly author of *Learning AI Native Software Engineering*) presents a production-minded, repeatable workflow for using AI to build AI: two agents with different jobs — a **coding agent** (Claude Code, but multiple work) as the *builder* that writes and changes a **product agent's** codebase, and the product agent feeding back evals and full/thinking traces so the builder can self-improve — targeting two failure classes, bad performance on **evals** and on **live data**. The eval backbone is a **golden dataset** (input → expected output, where expected output is often "call this tool / with this parameter / in this chain") treated as a test suite for a non-deterministic system, plus **scorers** producing an accuracy number for baseline/regression tracking; a naive tool-less Mastra "math agent" checked by a contains-ground-truth evaluator scores 18% (only simple arithmetic answerable from weights). For the evals class he built **AutoAgent**, inspired by Andrej Karpathy's "auto research" loop (a coding agent mutating ML code/hyperparameters improves a model): it hill-climbs the product agent's evals as **one hypothesis per git branch** (one failure class at a time), keeping the branch when evals improve and **rolling back to the previous branch** on regression, with a global cross-run **memory file**, per-iteration `reports.md`, and a full readable/steerable **change log** of hypotheses; the optimization job is a markdown file (objective, target repo, metrics, context); a hard human-in-the-loop **guardrail** forbids the coding agent from editing the golden dataset/scorers just to pass. Reported lift without cheating: naive agent **18%→83%** in ~10 iterations, a real production agent **67%→86%** in ~10 iterations (found edge cases, improved system prompt + tool descriptions, fixed tool logic; now in production), and **+10%** on an already-human-optimized agent (improvements humans hadn't found). For the live-data class a second loop collects production traces (interaction, tool usage, latency, tokens) plus user thumbs/comments or SME trace annotations; once enough accumulate (114-trace demo, downloaded as JSON), an agent workflow packaged as a **skill** clusters the failures, does an **adversarial review** + **root-cause analysis** (the coding agent has code + per-trace access), and emits a markdown report (clusters, trace IDs, root causes, fixes — e.g. "URLs not hyperlinked"). Humans **triage/validate with SMEs** (fix-now/later/never, discard false positives/intended behavior/unhelpful feedback), and — the durable step — every **validated** failure cluster is folded back into the **golden dataset** and scorers so the fix becomes a permanent regression test ("self-healing evals"), run ~once per sprint (split into several reports at 10,000+ traces); complex clusters are handed to AutoAgent to build draft PRs. Both loops are enabled by **Harness Engineering**: the spec-driven environment (each failure mode → a spec), quality gates (lint, unit tests, evals, LLM code review), context engineering, and observability built around the coding agent so it can change code, validate its own changes, and propose new ones reliably. Created two concepts — optimize-an-agent-with-a-branch-per-hypothesis-coding-agent-loop (Agents, Evaluation, Coding Agents; the AutoAgent git-branch hill-climb with rollback/memory/change-log and anti-gaming guardrail) and promote-validated-live-trace-failure-clusters-into-the-golden-dataset (Evaluation, Agents; the live-data cluster→root-cause→SME-triage→fold-into-golden-dataset self-healing-eval loop) — carving the two distinct durable ideas against existing coverage (Mutagent's agentic-AI-engineer offline/online loop, Feizi's route-to-right-layer + regression-aware learning, Chawla/Koul's replay test cases, the triage-agent and golden-dataset concepts) by owning the concrete git-branch search algorithm + anti-gaming rule and the promotion-into-eval-set policy, cross-referenced rather than duplicated. Integrated into Evaluation (both loops as an offline+live-data synthesis paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source), Agents (build-agents-with-agents synthesis paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source), and Coding Agents (Harness-Engineering synthesis paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source); concept-index lists the AutoAgent concept under Agents/Coding Agents/Evaluation and the promotion concept under Agents/Evaluation; index.md Current Emphasis lead + one log.md ingest entry. - `akk6KRlcwW4` | [OpenClaw in Your Hand: Building a Physical AI Terminal - Lech Kalinowski, Callstack](../sources/20260628_akk6KRlcwW4.md) | 2026-06-28 | transcript: `raw/20260628_akk6KRlcwW4/akk6KRlcwW4.en-orig.vtt` | Lech Kalinowski (PhD physicist, Callstack technological incubator) built "Vault," a text-first, dual-display handheld AI terminal as a calm, distraction-free remote control for an OpenClaw agent running on his NVIDIA DGX Spark. Hardware: an ESP32-S3 dual-core microcontroller with keyboard, encoder, a fast emissive OLED "live" surface (dynamic typed/streamed text), and a slow bistable e-paper "content" surface (final render committed on Enter), all on one lithium-polymer cell, with a substantial power-management unit added after two boards were blown up in prototyping. Four modes switch with a single slash command — internal **shell** (settings, Wi-Fi, config), **assist**, **control** (drive OpenClaw), and an **LLM-native RPG** — and the device surfaces an agent's thinking and tool calls in real time across the two surfaces. The durable architecture lesson is **"keep the model off the metal"**: the MCU runs only a tiny fast firmware doing I/O and rendering (fixed static buffers of pre-allocated one-bit images, deliberately *no markdown engine and no `malloc` on the MCU*), while a dependency-free Python backend owns all inference, autonomous agents, and a game engine — serving an open-source ~120B model (gpt-oss:120b) via NVIDIA TensorRT-LLM exposed OpenAI-style behind an **LLM proxy** (needed because not every open model matches the OpenAI API shape). Example agentic use: typed "write a Java example," OpenClaw + the LLM wrote and stored it locally; also checked DGX Spark disk space. Fault tolerance ("bulletproof") falls out of keeping the device thin — OLED down → e-paper works, keyboard down → encoder, Wi-Fi down → local shell. The second durable idea is design-level: the RPG tracks **narrative state, not numbers** — no HP or dice, just LLM-tracked mood, NPCs, memory, and generated worlds/characters/maps (rendered as one-bit matrices), across four worlds (cyberpunk "neon abyss," Witcher-like fantasy, deep-space "the void") — with the explicit takeaway "try narrative, context matters, and in general not numbers." War stories each cost ~a day: software I2C wiring, a GPIO-13 silent failure (moved ports), a power regulator that kept killing the fragile OLED/e-paper (weeks to source replacements), and a cheap noisy encoder needing added pull-ups/capacitors. Numbers: ~130 commits over ~3 months, 2 displays, 4 modes, 4 worlds, 16 classes; framed as "basically a Game Boy" to play with LLMs, with a provisional patent filed for the calm text-first niche. Created two concepts — keep-inference-off-the-microcontroller-thin-device-over-a-backend (Edge Inference, Infrastructure; the firmware-does-rendering / backend-does-inference architecture with the OpenAI-style proxy adapter) and design-llm-native-experiences-around-narrative-state-not-numbers (Agents, Generative Media; represent an LLM-driven experience's state as narrative context rather than a numeric rules engine) — integrated into Edge Inference (thin-client-vs-tiny-model synthesis paragraph + Key Concept + source), Infrastructure (self-hosting-to-a-pocket-terminal synthesis paragraph + Key Concept + source), Agents (state-representation companion to the runtime-NPC pattern + Key Concept + source), and Generative Media (Key Concept + source); concept-index lists the thin-client concept under Edge Inference and Infrastructure and the narrative-state concept under Agents and Generative Media; index.md Current Emphasis lead + one log.md ingest entry. - `dRmWYHuIJxM` | [We Cut 94% of AI Coding Tokens With a Local Code Index - Rajkumar Sakthivel, Tesco](../sources/20260628_dRmWYHuIJxM.md) | 2026-06-28 | transcript: `raw/20260628_dRmWYHuIJxM/dRmWYHuIJxM.en-orig.vtt` | Rajkumar Sakthivel (LLM-infrastructure engineer at Tesco) recounts how his and a collaborator's AI-coding bill jumped ~£15→£200 in a month with no usage change, and reframes the problem: the money was in oversized **input context**, not "AI thinking" (output) — a typical query sent ~45,000 tokens when only ~5,000 mattered ("like ordering a pizza and paying for extra nine pizzas you don't eat"). The "most important slide": **~90% of AI-coding cost is input, ~10% is output**, so cutting output 75% saves only ~8% of the total while cutting input 94% saves ~61% ("fix the input"); restated at the end as models ≈30% of cost vs context ≈70%. Three fixes underdeliver: prompt tweaks ("be short") change nothing because the 45K tokens are already charged before the model reads the instruction; model settings (max tokens, temperature) change output not input; output compression works but is capped since output is ~10% of cost. His fix (open-sourced as **Code Context Engine / CCE**) is a **local retrieval layer between the codebase and the coding agent** returning only relevant pieces via a five-step pipeline: (1) AST-aware chunking (functions/classes/methods, not random chunks), (2) *parallel* hybrid search — semantic-by-meaning + keyword-by-exact-name, combined (semantic misses exact names, keyword misses related ideas like "login flow" vs "sign in"; each misses ~1 in 4 alone, ~1 in 10 together), (3) compression (function name + description, 50 lines → 5), (4) a **call-graph relationship layer** (which function calls which, so one hit surfaces connected code across files), (5) a **relevance gate** scoring every result and dropping low ones ("no bad context"). The relevance gate was the hard part — a bad result gives a "confident wrong answer… worse than no answer"; an LLM self-judge was too slow (+2-3 s/query) and a fixed threshold too crude (short queries score low on perfect matches), so a simple weighted formula (50% meaning + 30% keyword + 20% recency) with an adaptive limit ran in ~0.4 ms with no extra AI calls — "simple formula beats the complex model most of the time." Everything runs locally (nothing to the cloud), one shared index + memory serves Claude Code/Cursor/Copilot so the codebase is "explained once." Benchmarks/caveats (honest): FastAPI (53 files, 20 questions) 83K→4.9K tokens/question (94%, or 523/q with compression) at ~90% right-code accuracy; 247 real queries saved 12.4M tokens (~$186), 84% from the search layer; but 94% is worst-case vs a full-file baseline (real tools are already smarter), and on a 396-file mixed codebase recall "dropped almost zero" — it works when files each do one thing, struggles when files do many. Created two concepts — cut-coding-agent-cost-by-fixing-the-input-not-the-model-or-output (Context Engineering, Coding Agents; the input-dominates-cost economic argument) and insert-a-local-code-index-retrieval-layer-between-codebase-and-agent (Coding Agents, Retrieval; the CCE five-step pipeline + relevance-gate heuristic + caveats) — carved against existing coverage (Treat-Embeddings-as-Cached-Compute, Share-Codebase-Indexes-Merkle-Tree, Hybrid-Retrieval, Native-Tool-Integration-vs-Bolted-On) by owning the cost-asymmetry framing and the local-tool-agnostic-index-plus-relevance-gate mechanism. Integrated into Context Engineering (cost-of-curation synthesis paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source), Coding Agents (retrieval-vs-model cost synthesis paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source), and Retrieval (code-specific hybrid-retrieval synthesis paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source); concept-index lists the cost concept under Context Engineering/Coding Agents and the code-index concept under Coding Agents/Context Engineering/Retrieval; index.md Current Emphasis lead + one log.md ingest entry. Selected one new processed source `dRmWYHuIJxM`, next unprocessed by upload_date desc / video_id asc in the 2026-06-28 cluster (after `akk6KRlcwW4`; among the remaining lowercase-initial IDs 'd'(100) > 'a'(97), so `dRmWYHuIJxM` follows the `a…` IDs; the cluster's remaining IDs after this are `uiP88SpCi1Q`, `vh2VGuQ3zhY`, `whue9_YquGA`); 331 of 476 rows in processed-sources. - `uiP88SpCi1Q` | [Your Agent Is Wasting Tokens and You Don't Know It - Erik Hanchett, AWS](../sources/20260628_uiP88SpCi1Q.md) | 2026-06-28 | transcript: `raw/20260628_uiP88SpCi1Q/uiP88SpCi1Q.en-orig.vtt` | Erik Hanchett (senior developer advocate, AWS) gives a ~6-min lightning talk with **five code-level levers to cut agent token cost** after a production agent's bill was "not good… because it was doing too much" — not because the model was bad. All five operate on the agent loop's per-request message assembly using AWS **Strands** agents (mechanisms are framework-agnostic), and none require changing prompts or switching models. **(1) Cache the system prompt** — Strands `cache_prompt = default` sends the full system prompt on the first call and a "much reduced" cached reference on every subsequent call; tool prompts and messages can be cached too. **(2) Route by difficulty** — don't use the most expensive model for everything; use a newer frontier model for a very difficult task, a cheaper model (Claude Haiku) for something simple, Claude Sonnet in between, via an `if` statement or even "another model that's very cheap" that decides which model to use. **(3) Offload the tool result** — store a large tool result locally or in the cloud and keep only a summary, so the result "isn't added into the context every time the tool loops or every time the agent loops." **(4) Cap tool loops** — without a max-iterations cap a tool can run "10, 20 times… an infinite loop, which would be very bad for your token usage," so always set max iterations, and run observability before deploy to see each tool's loop count and duration. **(5) Trim the history** — a multi-turn conversation's whole history is re-sent each call ("hundreds, if not thousands, of tokens"); Strands' `SlidingWindowConversationManager` sends only the last N (≈10) messages, with the lost-early-context tradeoff mitigated by summarizing older turns into the window. The unifying model: the loop re-transmits system prompt + tool results + history every iteration, so token spend compounds with loop length unless each re-sent part is shrunk and the loop count is bounded. Created two concepts — shrink-the-per-step-payload-the-agent-loop-re-sends (Context Engineering, Agents; the caching + tool-result offload + sliding-window levers, framed as reducing what the loop re-transmits) and route-each-request-to-the-cheapest-sufficient-model-by-difficulty (Inference, Agents; the difficulty-tier model routing lever) — carved against a heavily-covered area (KV-cache-hit-rate, agent-swarm-KV-working-sets, context-window-editing, offload-long-horizon-state, frequent-intentional-compaction, verification-guardrails-downshift, abstract-LLM-routing-API, cut-coding-agent-cost-by-fixing-the-input) by owning the per-step re-transmission cost model and the difficulty-triggered routing policy, cross-referenced rather than duplicated; the max-iterations cap was folded into topic synthesis via the existing contain-retry-amplification concept rather than made a third page. Integrated into Context Engineering (loop-re-transmission synthesis paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source), Inference (per-request model-routing synthesis paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source), and Agents (payload×steps cost-model synthesis paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source); concept-index lists the payload concept under Agents/Context Engineering and the routing concept under Agents/Inference; index.md Current Emphasis lead + one log.md ingest entry. Selected one new processed source `uiP88SpCi1Q`, next unprocessed by upload_date desc / video_id asc in the 2026-06-28 cluster (after `dRmWYHuIJxM`; among remaining lowercase-initial IDs 'u'(117) > 'd'(100), so `uiP88SpCi1Q` follows the `d…` ID; the cluster's remaining IDs after this are `vh2VGuQ3zhY`, `whue9_YquGA`); 332 of 476 rows in processed-sources. - `vh2VGuQ3zhY` | [The 100-Tool Agent Is a Trap - Sohail Shaikh & Ankush Rastogi, Prosodica](../sources/20260628_vh2VGuQ3zhY.md) | 2026-06-28 | transcript: `raw/20260628_vh2VGuQ3zhY/vh2VGuQ3zhY.en-orig.vtt` | Sohail Shaikh and Ankush Rastogi (Prosodica) diagnose the **"Fat Agent"** anti-pattern — loading every tool's name, description, and JSON schema into the system prompt on every request — and benchmark its collapse as the catalog grows: tool-selection accuracy falls ~78% at 10 tools → ~40% at ~100 → **13.6% at 741 tools** (≈1 correct in 8), a 741-tool catalog costs **~127,000 tokens/request** before the user's question, and time-to-first-token past ~500 tools can exceed **5 seconds**. The failure is structural ("it fails because every request is forced to carry the entire catalog," not because one tool is badly written) and rooted in **lost-in-the-middle** (models attend to the beginning/end of long context, so schemas packed in the middle are used unreliably). Fix: a **Semantic Tool Router** = "RAG for tools" + **Just-in-Time Context Injection** — embed each tool description offline into a vector index (Chroma/Pinecone/Qdrant), then at runtime embed the query with the same model, nearest-neighbor/cosine search returns the **top-K (K≈3–5)** closest tools, and only those schemas are injected. The routed agent stays **above ~83%** across the same catalog sizes with near-flat latency and **~99% fewer tool-context tokens**, echoing Anthropic's reported **150k→2k** (98.7%) drop from on-demand MCP tool loading; the router also *removes* the wrong tools from the choice set, cutting cross-tool confusion. Threshold: <20 tools a router may be unnecessary (static loading fine for 10–15), past ~50 tools in production it's justified — but tool confusion can appear with only a few tools, long before 100. Evaluation: measure four things (tool-selection accuracy, TTFT, input tokens/request, cost per 1,000 calls) over the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard, Skills-bench-style scenarios, and synthetic tool pools scaled to 10/50/100/200/1041 tools, running identical queries fat vs. routed; **sweep K at 3/5/10 and pick the smallest K meeting the accuracy target (K=5 default)**. Three failure modes: router miss (fallback: widen K, second retrieval pass, or broader tool group); weak descriptions → weak embeddings (write intent/action/entities in users' words); rare tools never score high (monitor misses, rewrite, re-embed on change). Production checklist: lock tools (name/description/schema/owner/version), build index, write router, wire the agent's tool list to the router (not a hard-coded catalog), evaluate at K=3/5/10, monitor (log selected tools, final calls, failures, fallback usage). Ecosystem: Aurelio Labs semantic-router, Toolbench, BFCL, the Anthropic MCP write-up, MCP Zero (thousands of tools across many servers), vector stores Pinecone/Qdrant/Chroma. Closing: "If your agent starts failing as tools are added, it doesn't automatically mean your prompts are bad — it may mean the architecture is asking the model to solve the wrong problem." Created two concepts — fat-agent-tool-overload-collapses-accuracy-and-latency (Agents, Tools, Context Engineering; the benchmarked accuracy/latency/cost collapse + lost-in-the-middle root cause + routing threshold) and tune-a-tool-router-with-k-sweep-and-guard-its-failure-modes (Tools, Evaluation; the four eval metrics, K-sweep, and three failure modes + production checklist) — and strengthened the existing retrieve-tool-descriptions-before-loading-large-tool-catalogs concept (previously AWS-only) with this second source for the semantic-router / RAG-for-tools mechanism, the 150k→2k signal, and MCP Zero. Integrated into Tools (Fat-Agent/semantic-router synthesis paragraph + 3 Key Concepts + source), Agents (tool-surface scaling-limit paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source), and Evaluation (tool-router eval-methodology synthesis + 1 Key Concept + source); concept-index lists fat-agent under Agents/Tools/Context Engineering and tune-router under Tools/Evaluation; index.md Current Emphasis lead + one log.md ingest entry. - `whue9_YquGA` | [Building an Autonomous Engineering Org - Angie Jones, Agentic AI Foundation](../sources/20260628_whue9_YquGA.md) | 2026-06-28 | transcript: `raw/20260628_whue9_YquGA/whue9_YquGA.en-orig.vtt` | Angie Jones recounts leading the transformation of Block's 3,500-person engineering org into an autonomous/agentic one. Core diagnosis: **adoption is not impact** — within months ~90% of engineers used Goose (Block's internal coding agent, the MCP-client reference implementation, built with Anthropic as MCP design partners) and Claude Code, and metrics + token bills proved it, yet "features certainly weren't making it to our customers any faster" because usage was stuck at IDE-level autocomplete/chat (three enablement phases: experimentation, adoption, impact). Two durable organizational tools. (1) An agentic-engineering **maturity model** (reorganized with help from Steve Yegge's "Gas Town" article) that ranks each engineer by their *delegation/orchestration relationship* with agents, not usage: Stage 0 no AI; Stage 1 autocomplete only, never agent mode; Stage 2 chatting with agents but no PRs; Stage 3 delegate tasks + review output; Stage 4 multiple agents in parallel; Stage 5 ("final boss") delegate complete tasks with shippable results and no human hand-holding — the bulk of 3,500 engineers sat at Stage 1–2, target was Stage 5. (2) A leverage strategy grounded in the **1-9-90 rule** ("1990 rule": 1% create, 9% interact, 90% consume): rather than upskill all 3,500, hand-pick a ~1% **AI champions** cohort (~50 engineers, not volunteers, ≥30% time, resilient to non-deterministic AI) from the most critical repos across every corner (Square, Cash App, Afterpay, Tidal; front/back/mobile/data/infra) and every repo shape (legacy monorepos, small services, mobile apps) to pressure-test what scales, and have them make the shared repos **AI-ready** — `AGENTS.md`/`CLAUDE.md` context files, rules-as-guardrails, slash commands + later agent skills, an enabled AI code reviewer with instructions, PR attribution, monorepo root-and-service inheritance, web≠mobile — so agent delegation "felt native to how people already work" for the 90% (bottom-up per-team convergence beat top-down "AI or die" mandate that caused fatigue). She then walks the org up the ladder: hands-off delegation from where requirements live (Jira/Linear/GitHub issues + `@goose` in Slack — full bug discussion→diagnosis→3 options→fix→PR in ~5 min; agents became sprint members), 3-month results (AI-authored code +69%, reported time savings +37%, automated PRs 21×), Stage 4 multi-agent parallelism with a **Codex auto-fix review loop** (another agent fixes Codex-flagged issues and commits before humans review) and **isolated cloud workspaces** (agents were colliding and choking laptop CPU/memory), a **Builder Bot** in-house orchestrator, and a machine-readable **company world model** over ~25,000 repos so agents plan across codebases — reaching Stage 5 where anyone could @Build-A-Bot in Slack to ship a fix without GitHub. Closing caveat (deliberately unresolved): the autonomy push was followed by **layoffs**, and she leaves the ethical/strategic "where are we heading… are we sure this is where we want to end up?" question open. Created two concepts — stage-agentic-engineering-adoption-with-a-delegation-maturity-model (Coding Agents, Workflows; the Stage 0–5 delegation ladder + adoption≠impact framing) and drive-org-wide-agentic-adoption-through-champions-and-ai-ready-repos (Coding Agents, Workflows; the 1-9-90 champions + make-shared-repos-AI-ready leverage strategy) — carved against a saturated adoption area (measure-ai-transformation-by-outcomes, measure-feature-adoption-not-shipping-velocity, on-mixed-ability-ai-teams-specialists-should-enable-not-do, universal-ai-adoption-changes-engineering-coordination, institutionalize-knowledge-infrastructure-for-ai-adoption, human-control-planes-turn-agent-swarms-into-manageable-organizations) by owning the per-engineer capability *ladder* and the org-scale champions-and-repo-substrate rollout mechanism; the Builder Bot orchestrator, company world model, Codex auto-fix loop, and cloud workspaces were folded into topic synthesis rather than made extra pages. Integrated into Coding Agents (org-transformation synthesis paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source) and Workflows (organizational-adoption synthesis paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source); concept-index lists both concepts under Coding Agents and Workflows; index.md Current Emphasis lead + one log.md ingest entry. - `4uFVSLgD2Q4` | [Agents in Production: How OpenGov Built and Scaled OG Assist - Gabe De Mesa, OpenGov](../sources/20260626_4uFVSLgD2Q4.md) | 2026-06-26 | transcript: `raw/20260626_4uFVSLgD2Q4/4uFVSLgD2Q4.en-orig.vtt` | Gabe De Mesa (engineer, OpenGov AI Agents team) walks the production architecture of **OG Assist**, an agent surface embedded as a nav-bar button across OpenGov's government ERP products (budgeting, procurement, asset management, permitting) where each product team contributes its own tools and skills; the agent can also see and act on the current screen. Two load-bearing bets. (1) **Own the agent loop on Effect-TS**: they started on **LangGraph**, which "was fine until the team really started to scale and our use cases started to evolve," then moved to a self-owned Effect-native loop for full control, so Effect's tracing/spans, structured concurrency, logging, and dependency-injected model hot-swap propagate uniformly across the whole loop "out of the box" ("you can't scale what you can't see"); the Effect AI package supplies `chat`/`language model` primitives (`stream text` from a prompt), DI lets them swap the model without changing the loop, and tools plug in the Effect way (define tool → add to a tool kit → register the tool kit with the language model). (2) **Adopt Google's A2A spec as an internal front-end/back-end contract**: they modeled back-end agent routes/schema on the A2A **agent card** (name, description, capabilities) that both sides consume and produce — not to cross an ownership boundary but because "this rigorous spec really helped drive our development and drive alignment"; A2A extensions (metadata, A2UI) let it grow. Surrounding already-covered production practices reinforced: thumbs up/down feedback + automated evals in CI against real completions ("shipping is the start, not the finish"), deterministic human-in-the-loop interrupts with accept/reject UI before mutating tool calls, on-demand ephemeral sandboxes for agent-written code/files (PDF gen, torn down after), rolling summarization + recall over the summary for long context, runtime generative UI from registered primitives, and internal Claude/Cursor use for developer velocity. Created two concepts — own-the-agent-loop-on-a-typed-effects-runtime (Agents, Infrastructure; the framework-graduation + Effect substrate benefit) and adopt-a2a-as-an-internal-front-end-back-end-contract (Agents, Tools; A2A-spec-as-internal-contract, qualifying choose-a2a-and-mcp-by-ownership-boundary) — carved against a saturated area (existing A2A concepts own cross-org interop/discovery; existing sandbox/human-approval/long-context/generative-UI/tools-and-skills concepts own the surrounding practices), which were folded into topic synthesis rather than duplicated. Integrated into Agents (own-loop + A2A synthesis paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source), Tools (A2A-as-internal-contract paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source), and Infrastructure (observability-out-of-the-box paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source); concept-index lists own-loop under Agents/Infrastructure and A2A-contract under Agents/Tools; index.md Current Emphasis lead + one log.md ingest entry. - `ZRM_TfEZcIo` | [Turn 10,994 Notes Into Memory - Paul Iusztin, Decoding AI & Louis-François Bouchard, Towards AI](../sources/20260626_ZRM_TfEZcIo.md) | 2026-06-26 | transcript: `raw/20260626_ZRM_TfEZcIo/ZRM_TfEZcIo.en-orig.vtt` | Paul Iusztin (Decoding AI) and Louis-François Bouchard (Towards AI) demo an **"AI Research OS"**: a personal memory layer that sits between a "second brain" (10,000+ notes across Obsidian, Readwise, Notion, Google Drive) and any agent harness (Codex, Claude Code), open-sourced at `github.com/iusztinpaul/ai-research-os-workshop` as a Claude Code plugin. Two durable ideas. (1) A tool-selection **ladder** keyed on durability + ownership, not speed: a throwaway answer → Google/ChatGPT (but you depend on their architecture and lose context each session); a one-off change/article → Codex/Claude Code; revisitable content → NotebookLM (powerful but you don't own it, not agent-native, weak for coding since browser-based); production scale → a RAG/vector-DB pipeline (powerful but needs infra, not human-inspectable); a personalized, inspectable, compounding "Wikipedia" you own → build your own file-based research OS (more setup than opening Claude Code). Underlying principle: the bottleneck is not how much context you feed but how you *reuse* it — "the context window becomes everything… and when you stop the conversation, it loses everything" — so the need is memory + context management. (2) A **file-based three-layer wiki with progressive-disclosure retrieval**: "forget the infrastructure you think you need" (vector DBs, knowledge graphs, semantic search) and build on plain Markdown + a reference index — `raw/` immutable source copies, an `index.yaml` catalog (per-source summaries + metadata like origin/title/authors/date; example held 10 sources + 38 wiki pages), and an LLM-generated `wiki/` of derivatives (per-source executive summaries, concepts, entities, comparisons, notes, open questions). Retrieval reads cheapest-first: index → source executive summary (often enough, then stop) → wiki derivatives → raw source only as a last resort, so an agent rarely loads a whole raw document. The wiki is "alive" — every question spawns a derivative file and is logged, so it evolves from *use* not only ingestion; the raw personal vault (Obsidian, organized with Tiago Forte's PARA method) stays an immutable snapshot the LLM never edits, and work is scoped per-project via a deep-research pass ("the project is the work, and your second brain is the research"). The deep-research loop itself (evolved V1 golden-links+web→static `research.md` → V2 aimed at the second brain, topic-only input → V3 +wiki layer): one orchestrator generates N questions per round from topic + scraped seed context, sub-agents each own a question using Gemini grounded in Google, produce executive summaries, orchestrator aggregates without exploding context (3 rounds × 6 queries → ~40-50 links), then a ranking algorithm scores each source vs the topic, fully scrapes only top-K, keeps summaries for the rest; a demo depth control (light 1×3 / fast 2×3 / deep) manages token cost (deep takes ~10-20 min). Demos ingest a brain-dump on agentic harness engineering, three harness repos (opencode, "Pi", Hermes → per-repo notes + cross-repo comparisons, "super useful if you want to write your own harness"), and three arbitrary links with zero setup (git + curl); Bright Data parses SPAs. By-design a builder workflow (not a product); acknowledged weaknesses: source provenance/freshness is hard ("hard to know which sources are outdated or weak or strong") and next priorities are stronger linting + better memory compaction. Created two concepts — file-based-research-wiki-with-progressive-disclosure-retrieval (Context Engineering, Retrieval; the raw/index/wiki-derivative store + cheapest-first read path + alive-from-use property, a no-vector-DB personal memory) and choose-the-research-tool-by-reuse-and-ownership (Workflows, Retrieval; the chat→coding-agent→NotebookLM/RAG→own-file-wiki ladder with tradeoffs). Integrated into Context Engineering (durable-knowledge-lives-in-files synthesis paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source), Retrieval (no-vector-DB personal-memory paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source), and Workflows (research-tool-ladder + research-vs-work synthesis paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source); concept-index lists the wiki concept under Context Engineering/Retrieval and the tool-selection concept under Workflows/Retrieval; index.md Current Emphasis lead + one log.md ingest entry. - `ij-AU9dpJjc` | [Stop Writing Tone Instructions. Layer Them. - Isadora Martin-Dye, Isadora & Co](../sources/20260626_ij-AU9dpJjc.md) | 2026-06-26 | transcript: `raw/20260626_ij-AU9dpJjc/ij-AU9dpJjc.en-orig.vtt` | Isadora Martin-Dye (founder, Isadora & Co) runs a 225-year-old Virginia wedding venue and built the AI agents behind it (a couples-facing assistant "In Bloom," a multi-venue version, a personal AI companion, and Thread Light for families of missing people). Thesis: a brand voice that survives real users "isn't an instruction you write once — it's an architecture." A single "write in our brand's voice" system prompt holds on **the happy path** but breaks on "**turn 21**," the first case the examples never anticipated, because it is being asked to do **four completely different jobs**. Fix: a **four-layer prompt stack** composed in a **fixed order through one assembly function** ("hard rules first, task to last," analogized to Google Maps routing). **(1) Immutable identity** — hard constraints "the brand structurally cannot say," overridable by nothing below (mandatory AI self-disclosure "in its very first response… not if asked, but before they ask"; a physical-presence boundary — "you are software, you do not have a body," so "I'd love to show you around" is forbidden but "the team would love to host you for a tour" is allowed; cross-product proof from Thread Light forbidding "confirmed/identified/matched/proven/linked/solved," since "match" said to a grieving parent is "the single most damaging thing a product could ever do"). **(2) Situational mode** — real-time who-you're-talking-to and what-they're-going-through signals "built into the prompt before it runs" (a coordinator gets "colleague not customer" framing; a couple in grief gets gentleness, "never quote soft-context notes verbatim"), with intra-prompt ordering that renders tone notes *before* numeric constraints because reversing it "makes the prose feel mechanically slotted." **(3) Example-anchored voice** — the tone guide/phrase list/dials, "the induction pack," "where most teams start and stop," good on the happy path but "not the right tool for guarantees." **(4) Post-generation veto** — "the only layer that actually reads what came out" and "the only part that isn't a prompt": a *soft flag* ("honesty inspector") and a *hard reject* ("numbers guard") that checks specifics against an allow list so an offered-but-booked date "doesn't ship." The load-bearing distinction: the first three layers are probabilistic **instructions** ("asking nicely and hoping") that "usually" work but "a prompt will eventually lose," while the fourth is deterministic **permission** ("checking, and you are sure") — needed because "usually" is fine for a slightly off-brand sentence but "not fine when the model is confidently inventing a fact a real person is about to act on — a date, a price, a policy, a promise" (a warm confident offer of a fabricated date is "worse than a cold one… a disappointment with a 48-hour delay"). Cost asymmetry justifies the veto (false positive = a double-checked response; false negative = "a hallucinated number or a privacy violation that ships"), and she chooses deterministic pattern-matching over a probabilistic classifier ("determinism over coverage"). **Multi-tenant seam** = "calling one function" (Layer 1 shared, Layers 2-3 per-tenant); a silently-defaulted brand-identity field caused a white-label leak ("every venue shipped as sage@hawthornemanner.com"), fixed by the principle "identity must never have a default. A missing brand identity is a crash, not a fallback. It must fail loud." Created two concepts — layer-brand-voice-into-composable-prompt-tiers (Context Engineering, Product Strategy; the four-tier stack + fixed-order assembly + load-bearing ordering + fail-loud multi-tenant identity) and gate-generated-output-with-a-deterministic-veto (Evaluation, Context Engineering; instructions-probabilistic-vs-permission-deterministic, the soft-flag/hard-reject + allow-list check, cost asymmetry, and the determinism-over-coverage trade-off) — carved against a saturated prompt/guardrail area by owning the *persona layering* and the *outbound-content deterministic gate* framing, cross-referencing rather than duplicating enforce-deterministic-guardrails-around-sensitive-tool-calls (tool-call boundary), wrap-agent-completion-in-an-automatic-deterministic-verification-gate (coding-agent completion), and separate-generation-and-verification-prompts-or-models. Integrated into Context Engineering (prompt-composition-as-architecture paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source), Evaluation (deterministic-output-veto paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source), and Product Strategy (voice-is-the-product / high-stakes-relationship-brand paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source); concept-index lists the layering concept under Context Engineering/Product Strategy and the veto concept under Evaluation/Context Engineering; index.md Current Emphasis lead + one log.md ingest entry. - `AN65uc645mE` | [200 Million Patient Interactions Later — Vivek Muppalla, Hippocratic AI](../sources/20260819_AN65uc645mE.md) | 2026-08-19 | transcript: `raw/20260819_AN65uc645mE/AN65uc645mE.en-orig.vtt` | Vivek Muppalla (head of engineering, Hippocratic AI) walks the production stack behind 200M+ outbound clinical patient calls across 60+ health systems. Extracted three durable patterns: ASR as a decoder-only audio LLM conditioned on conversation context and domain vocabulary (fine-tuned Whisper V3 large turbo encoder + prosody-preserving conformer projector, finite candidate lists, training-time contextual biasing, single-word rescoring, >50% medical WER reduction); the Polaris "constellation" of 31 concurrent models (one thread-holding model + 30 specialists) kept inside the latency budget by a cheap per-specialist speak-up gate, with async verifiers on tool-call parameters/responses and offline verification for reversible actions; and eval sizing from consequence — 1% of 10,000 daily calls is 100 misrouted patients, ~450 tests to detect a 1% error rate at 99% confidence (~1,900 to see it 10x), 7,000+ clinicians running continuous evaluation, and grading on the human harm scale (correctness / no harm / minor / severe / death; 99.89% no-harm vs ~81% human). Also captured the latency-to-intelligence compounding flywheel, lossless inference optimizations (4-bit quantization, speculative decoding, KV-cache compression at >96% hit rate and 18x faster prefill), and the HEART empathy benchmark.
- `McknwOzbmyg` | [Shipping AI to a Million Patients Without an A/B Test — Jared Joselowitz, Ufonia](../sources/20260819_McknwOzbmyg.md) | 2026-08-19 | transcript: `raw/20260819_McknwOzbmyg/McknwOzbmyg.en-orig.vtt` | Jared Joselowitz (research engineer, Ufonia science team) builds the safety and evaluation stack behind **Dora**, a UK voice agent that phones patients for post-op follow-ups and pre-op checks (~200,000 real clinical calls across 20 hospitals; contracted to a million patients in two years; US launch live in two clinics with six more signed across four states). Because Dora asks about symptoms, gives advice, and answers questions, it "is what's called a medical device" and must be proven safe — regulation reducing to three questions (what does your software do / what could go wrong / how do you ensure it doesn't), of which the second is "quite huge" for a model talking to patients. Three durable ideas. (1) **The reactive loop is gone.** Shipping to patients removes three safety nets: no A/B test ("randomizing patients into a worse variant is unethical and often illegal"), no rollback ("once Dora says it, it's been said"), and no benchmark defense (a vendor's "92% on some benchmark… is not a defense at a post incident review"). Ship-to-5%-watch-the-dashboard-roll-back is "a very good playbook" whose hidden assumption is "it only works because you can afford to be wrong for an instance" — at patient scale 5% is "hundreds if not thousands of patients that have got unproven changes and undue care," and "the dashboards going red means that a patient was actually hurt." Replacement borrowed from self-driving's "millions of miles of simulations first": start from harm (hazards written with clinicians — missing a red flag such as sudden vision loss or severe pain, hallucinating a medical answer, ignoring patient distress; "20, 30, 40" documented), then run them in **Matrix**, a simulation framework (paper on arXiv) that "recreates a real clinical conversation but with no real patient in it." (2) **Validate both halves of the simulation loop.** **PatBot** is an LLM playing the patient (chosen over hired actors because "hired actors don't scale"), conditioned on a scenario that "defines exactly what the patient should try and do," grounded "in real clinical workflows and not abstract situations." Simulator validation ran in two steps: a script-adherence yes/no check that "helped us filter out a lot of maybe weaker models that didn't listen to instructions properly" (but "purely following instructions does not make a realistic patient"), then a **PPI (patient and public involvement) study** where real patients compared a real-doctor/real-patient conversation against a Dora/PatBot one — in **three of four sets** the majority thought the *simulated* patient was more realistic, with the key finding that "there is no single realistic patient" (verbose vs terse), so the goal is diverse personas. Grading is done by an LLM judge (spoken `BevJudge`) taking dialogue + expected behaviors + clinician-agreed hazard scenarios and returning pass/fail with a reason and "a structured output of which hazards were triggered"; it was validated on a **240-example corpus with ground truth, labeled by 10 clinicians from 10 clinical specialties**, coming out "at least on par, if not slightly better, than the real expert clinicians" (top model at paper time Gemini 2.5 Pro, **F1 0.96**) with "almost perfect **sensitivity**" — the metric that counts because "you would rather overcall hazards that aren't there than undercall hazards that are there." (3) **Optimize prompts against a cost matrix.** "Grading isn't technically improving the product," and hand-tuning cannot survive prompt brittleness — "formatting changes alone have been seen to swing benchmarks by 76 percentage points," and reordering few-shot examples "flips a model from near random, so near 50%, to near state-of-the-art." They use **GEPA** ("Jeppa," genetic Pareto, "from the same people who made DSPy"): define a metric, run the data, get failures, "get a very strong LLM to reflect on the failures and update the prompt automatically," repeat while keeping "a pareto frontier of the best prompts until your budget has been exhausted" — hours-to-days becomes 30-60 minutes, reproducible with "a very clear audit trail," and the residual work becomes data + feature engineering + "you define the actual metric along with the clinicians." The metric is explicitly "not a flat accuracy score… you give it a cost matrix": catching a present red flag is good, missing it "could be catastrophic," over-calling one is "just mildly annoying to the patient"; so the reward is shaped for sensitivity, and a clinician wanting a different objective need only "recompile the prompt." The full flywheel is real calls + manufactured synthetic edge cases (rare symptoms, mis-transcriptions) → optimized prompt → Matrix as a **simulation safety gate** → gated deploy, with every deployment producing more call data. Closing discipline: simulation is "necessary, but not sufficient" — it "only earns the right to actually try carefully" — as the fast, free inner loop, with real patients the outer loop and "the only real proof"; the crossing is staged (user testing → supervised clinical evaluation with clinicians at every step → monitored deployment), and "how much autonomy you allow the system to do depends on your evidence." The deliverable is traceability: "every call, every data set, and every pinned prompt, every judge verdict traces back to the exact hazard that it addresses… you don't ship the model, you ship the evidence." Portable takeaways: define what harm means for your product, "manufacture your rare but dangerous cases — don't wait for them," "make your evaluation metric your real cost function," and pin prompt versions with traces. New modalities add hazards but not a new method — voice brings backchanneling and interruptions where an agent cut off mid-safety-advice ("you must avoid bright lights") makes weak models "just forget about the safety advice and answer the next question" — but "the failure modes [change]. The framework doesn't… voice is just a new module in the same safety case." Created three concepts — replace-ship-and-rollback-with-hazard-first-simulation (Evaluation, Voice Agents, Healthcare Operations), validate-the-simulated-user-and-the-judge (Evaluation, Voice Agents), and optimize-prompts-against-an-asymmetric-cost-matrix (Evaluation, Workflows) — carved against a saturated simulation/judge/prompt-optimization area: existing pages own simulated personas as a technique (simulated-conversations-test-customer-facing-agents-before-launch, simulate-voice-agents-with-probabilistic-conversation-evals), judge calibration mechanics (calibrate-llm-judges-like-binary-classifiers), and evaluator quality as an optimizer dependency (evaluator-quality-is-a-dependency-of-prompt-optimization), while these three own the irreversibility-forces-hazard-first framing, the validate-*both*-models-against-humans demand, and the cost-matrix-as-objective move. Integrated into Evaluation (synthesis paragraph + 3 Key Concepts + source), Voice Agents (paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source), Healthcare Operations (paragraph + 3 Key Concepts + new open question + source), and Workflows (paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source); concept-index lists all three under Evaluation and Healthcare Operations, two under Voice Agents, one under Workflows; index.md Current Emphasis lead + one log.md ingest entry. Selected one new processed source `McknwOzbmyg`, the next unprocessed video by upload_date desc / video_id asc (2026-08-19 cluster; 'M' follows the already-processed 'A' of `AN65uc645mE`); 339 rows in processed-sources against a 537-record index.jsonl. VTT (~1154s / ~19-min single-speaker talk + slides + one recorded call demo) used rolling two-line captions deduped mentally by keeping each cue's last text line; ASR proper-noun fixes cross-checked against title/description — "Euphony"→Ufonia, "Jeppa"→GEPA, "Matched"→Matrix, "post instant review"→post-incident review; the judge name is kept as spoken (`BevJudge`) since neither title nor description spells it; no facts asserted beyond title/description/transcript.
- `UyyOoJmuATU` | [Healthcare's Agent Bytecode: X12 as the Harness for AI Agents — Vasant Kearney, Onlay](../sources/20260819_UyyOoJmuATU.md) | 2026-08-19 | transcript: `raw/20260819_UyyOoJmuATU/UyyOoJmuATU.en-orig.vtt` | Vasant Kearney (Onlay) describes an agentic execution layer for insurance claims, grounded in the healthcare EDI standard **X12** treated as part of the *harness* rather than as a file format. Framing: agentic healthcare work must reduce the cost of interacting with insurance or improve the patient experience (01:15-01:54); the lineage runs neuron → CNNs → large-scale unsupervised learning → "Attention Is All You Need" → chat interfaces → "the Claude codes, the Codexes," OpenClaw, Hermes agent, which is what introduces the execution layer (01:58-03:02). Harness analogy: "we have solved the handwritten digit problem" does not mean you can deposit a check — you still match other characters, other infrastructure, and the target account; claims have the same shape, many little AI steps between an eligibility check and money in the provider's bank, all inside strict confinements (03:06-04:36). Three durable ideas extracted. (1) **Ground agent actions in an existing domain transaction standard**: harness = "all the different nuts and bolts that surround this agentic reasoning" — memory, tools, checks, permissions, handoffs, evals — "but also in the context of healthcare and claims, it's X12" (08:31-09:03); like COBOL or TypeScript, "LLMs really thrive … when they're confined, they have clear limited values that they can predict, and X12 is exactly this," supplying "this contract between what you're trying to communicate and the insurance company" (09:04-09:40); a ~50-step objective propagates errors, so "it's very good to have something grounded that can be rejected to" (09:44-10:25); the balance point is that free reasoning over 50 multimodal steps is expensive/slow/error-multiplying while hardcoding makes "your code … explode to be just unmanageable" needing "a giant engineering team" (10:27-11:18); every lifecycle stage has an X12 correspondence — scheduling, treatment eligibility, documents (sometimes the x-ray *is* the document), submission, payment (12:36-13:20) — and post-submission runs 999 syntax ack → status update → optional verification call → EOB/**835** (18:28-18:55); the channel-collapse insight is that calling a payer "boils down to a transaction, an X12 transaction" (patient identification = **270**, imaging attachment = **275**), so phone, desktop, browser, and EDI are one transaction by different routes, with the bank/ACH leg structured but not X12 (13:29-14:26); and the standard is public — "this is not my schema. If you ask agents to make a schema for you you're going to get like all sorts of stuff" — so both a research agent and a new engineer can look every transaction up (14:39-15:25). (2) **Treat every external system of record as non-authoritative**: a payer's portal, phone system, X12 layer, and FHIR layer may be built by different teams or "even a different company that the insurance company contracts out," and "it doesn't mean that when an insurance company gives you an X12, it's true"; they disagree and "can all actually agree on the wrong information as well" — all three confirm coverage, you treat the patient, the claim is denied because the patient "wasn't covered during that time" — while payer idiosyncrasies about which surface is more reliable are learned over time (15:30-16:41); hence "boil all those transactions down to your own internal semi-correct X12. Correct until downstream evidence proves it otherwise," and any payer information "any time can be wrong, it can be updated later" (16:42-17:10). (3) **Give enterprise agents tiered database memory with an escape hatch**: "cloud code or codex, they use local memory, they write to your desktop. In enterprise healthcare, we can't really do this, so we do memory in a database, just so we have that logical separation" (07:23-07:38); partner/organizational plus user tiers work because multi-site staff "tend to do the same thing day after day," so a few words resolve intent per user (11:19-12:05); but persistent memory across chats and days "also introduce[s] bias" and can "steer them to do the exact same thing they did yesterday. That's a problem," so "any user can break out of this" (12:06-12:33). Also captured: multimodal flattening loses context — reducing an image to findings/anatomy/geometries before a downstream EHR model can drop what mattered "to some downstream procedure that … the upstream model wasn't aware of," and desktop-use shapes are "only obvious when you do this multimodal" (04:42-06:09); the execution-layer action surface (open DB queries and schema discovery, code queried against data, insurance transactions, phone calls, portals, EHR; write implications and at-minimum user logs on PMS desktops, 06:16-07:18); patient-side details (upfront cost estimate matters for experience; a CBCT and its slices can be the evidence the payer asks for; the claim is "a receipt of what you did … like the invoice," 17:13-18:25); and the closing posture "I'm fully AI pilled … but you should also be very AI skeptical" — "we make mistakes designing them. We might set them up to fail" — plus the cost rule against an overpowered model on "routine things that need to be done a thousand times a day" (19:00-20:02). Strengthened the existing concept treat-model-and-prompt-upgrades-as-regulated-migrations with his system-design version: "you can't just replace the model and assume it's going to be better. It's different," because benchmark wins do not transfer to "the way you've designed your system," so you "really have to redo everything from scratch" (07:40-08:23). Carved against a saturated harness/schema area by owning framings no page held: existing pages own harness *composition* (agent-harnesses-combine-model-tools-prompts-…), schema constraint as a *typed SDK / constrained decoding* technique (constrain-agent-effects-not-expression-with-a-typed-sdk, constrained-decoding-makes-small-model-tool-calls-production-usable, type-safe-agent-schemas-…), and telemetry normalization (normalize-network-telemetry-into-agent-readable-schemas), while these own *adopt the industry's published standard as the harness constraint and let it collapse action channels*, *no external surface is authoritative and agreement is not confirmation*, and *enterprise memory placement plus the personalization-steers-the-user hazard*. - `WJRdLNhrsLQ` | [How to build an AI-Native Health Company — Dan Feng, Maven Clinic](../sources/20260819_WJRdLNhrsLQ.md) | 2026-08-19 | transcript: `raw/20260819_WJRdLNhrsLQ/WJRdLNhrsLQ.en-orig.vtt` | Dan Feng describes Maven Clinic's ~2-year transition from a traditional technology company to an AI-native one. Maven Clinic is presented as the largest digital health platform for women and their families (maternity, fertility, parenting, menopause); the AI journey produced **Maven Intelligence**, "an orchestration layer across all our product to enable AI for everybody in this company and for our clients" (00:47-01:15). Framing: adoption is competitive, not optional — "even you choose not to, your competitors will do"; "tractors aren't to replace farmers, but the farmers who can operate the tractor will replace the ones who cannot" (01:18-01:43); and "there's no predefined playbook you can just follow and bingo, you become AI native" (01:46-01:59). Their three parts of AI-native: internal use wherever possible ("whenever you want to ask other people to do something for you, you should be saying with yourself I can use AI to do it"), AI in the product for user experience and operational cost, and culture/process/way-of-working, which he calls the most important (01:59-03:20). Adoption is segmented into early adopters (just enable tools and have them share), the majority (shared AI infrastructure, easy tools, consistent feedback), and the reluctant (understand concerns, "but more important, we should be crystal clear with them where the company is heading to"), with tool support following engineers rather than dictating — "last year, most of folks and Maven they are using Cursor. This year, a lot of them switch to Claude Code… We need to meet where they are" (03:22-04:35). Three durable ideas extracted as concepts. (1) **Commit at sprint scale when implementation is cheap**: the old weeks-to-months requirements phase existed because "implementation can be really expensive," but "we never get the sense and right in the beginning anyway for any of big projects," and now "with AI, building is super fast. It's probably couple minutes you can get it done. Argument is really expensive one" (06:58-07:47); so the one-year view is directional only and may assume "AI models can do anything you want in one year," the two-to-four-week sprint is the real commitment ("tell me what I need to deliver in this sprint"), and reversal is designed in — "if at the end of this sprint, they say, 'No, what we decided two weeks ago is wrong.' It's totally okay" (07:47-08:45); PRDs and TDDs shrink to "just a short one or two pages… serve as communication, so we can iterate on it" (08:45-09:01); and the casualty is the mid-term plan — "three months, six months. It's very hard to plan these days… I don't know what AI models will be capable in three months. There may be multiple releases already" (09:01-09:41). (2) **Sort failures by whether the user can retry**: traditional software "does what we implement there, no more, no less," but with GenAI "hallucination is there," and eliminating it "can be very costly. Sometimes is not necessary, either," so the first step is to "identify which failures is acceptable, which ones are not acceptable" (13:29-14:11); scheduling failing one in a thousand is tolerable because "the users really can just click the button again" (14:11-14:28), while reimbursement claims have zero tolerance because the error escalates in either direction — "if people ask of $200, we issue them 50 or they ask 50, we give them 200. Each case will cause a escalation right away" — so "we will use different models to review the same receipt. We only move forward if the results from different models agree with each other," with unresolved disagreement routed to a human agent as an explicitly "acceptable solution" (14:28-15:17). (3) **Earn release confidence from repeated runs and post-launch sampling**: hundreds of integration tests cover known use cases and keep growing, and passing once "is not good enough anymore, because the LLM can do different things. So, for each test case, we run it to many times. We consistently requires the high pass rate, like for example, 90% for all the time" (15:17-15:50); after launch an automated system evaluates "each conversation" against predefined rubrics while "a dedicated group" spot-checks conversations to decide "whether we need to come back to improve our systems, or our rubrics is too strict or too loose," with coverage raised to "like say 20%" for new feature launches, so that "we are really confident whenever we release something, although we know hallucination is there" (15:50-16:50). Also captured and filed into existing concepts rather than new pages: the coding-tool adoption ladder (start with unit tests and documentation because they are "very easy to verify and the risk is super low," use that phase to "construct our own rules, skills, and build our barriers," then push team-wide and investigate refusals; AI tools now do essentially all implementation while "engineers really focus on reviewing, architecturing, and evaluation," 09:44-10:57); and the code-review redesign at ~10x volume ("used to they probably write hundreds of lines code every day. These days, they can easily write like thousands") — author self-certification with retained accountability ("we let them merge, but we still hold them accountable"), a 500-line PR cap, stacked PRs for large features, AI review tools kept as a bet but not trusted alone, and the rubber stamp named as the worst outcome because it "just give us false confidence. We think we reviewed it, it's good, and we release it" (10:59-12:57). Those two went to route-each-change-to-the-proof-it-needs (the author-side implementation of per-change review routing) and ai-output-speed-can-overwhelm-review-capacity (a first-hand 10x-volume account with concrete policies), both of which already own the framing. Second-order org notes: senior engineers stopped delegating implementation because "they already figure out how to solve the problem. They just use AI to solve it instantly," so new hires must solve problems independently; hiring shifted toward product understanding since "the boundaries between PM and engineers is getting blur" and ambiguity handling "is where AI [falls] off"; performance reviews now ask "what do you have done for AI side?" (04:43-06:55). Stated but unreached goal: full lifecycle automation plus AI monitoring live traffic to "catch the issue early and automatically fix it… we're not there yet" (12:57-13:29). Integrated into Workflows (planning-horizon paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source), Coding Agents (adoption-ladder and review-redesign paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source), Evaluation (retryability and release-gate paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source), Product Strategy (planning-cadence paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source), and Healthcare Operations (fourth-position paragraph comparing all four healthcare sources' risk tiers + 2 Key Concepts + new open question + source); concept-index gained 11 grouped entries across Agents, Coding Agents, Evaluation, Healthcare Operations, Product Strategy, and Workflows; index.md Current Emphasis lead and one log.md ingest entry. - `XAsb7MIAzm8` | [Don't be data poor — Anuj Iravane, Anterior](../sources/20260819_XAsb7MIAzm8.md) | 2026-08-19 | transcript: `raw/20260819_XAsb7MIAzm8/XAsb7MIAzm8.en-orig.vtt` | Anuj Iravane (AI lead, Anterior — clinician-led AI for health plans, backed by Sequoia and NEA) describes how a vertical AI company builds eval datasets when it is contractually forbidden to keep the data those evals need. Framing: the work across prior authorization, payment integrity, and HEDIS measures is all "policy-guided decision-making over highly unstructured data" (00:16-00:59); the input is scanned fax bundles ("around 70% of medical communication still happens via fax") past 300 pages, with handwriting, tables, checkboxes, key-value pairs and images, from "a very long tail of rare … cases," modelling an entire clinical trajectory — "an observation through a very fuzzy lens over an entire person's lifespan" (00:59-02:02); the bar is "95% is not good enough," which is why Anterior "invest[s] very deeply in datasets and evals" (02:02-02:24). The constraint: the records are PHI and "we can't retain it, we can't reuse it, we can't even derive information from it," with redaction, anonymization, and derivative copies "a strict no-no, completely off the table," so "nothing really survives in any sort of dataset that we want to persist" (02:29-02:54) — "what do you do when the dataset you most need is also the data you're least allowed to keep" (02:54-03:00). Three durable ideas extracted. (1) **Generate eval data by reversing the inference workflow**: the forward task is unstructured data + policy → reasoning trace → label, so the reverse is "sampling a random label, figuring out a reasoning trace for that label, and then trying to generate data backwards from that" (05:00-05:48); it works because Anterior already models policies "explicitly as decision trees" in a symbolic representation adopted for execution accuracy and consistency (CPAP medical-necessity policy as the worked example), which yields "a way to kind of deterministically sample different reasoning traces for a given outcome" from "a much more uniform and effective prior distribution than what you'd normally get from an LLM" (05:53-07:18); the alternative fails on mode collapse, attributed to thin pre-training exposure and objectives "not incentivized for creativity or diversity … they're incentivized to be helpful systems" (04:32-04:50); coverage is the second argument — 200 real customer cases scoring 95% "doesn't really tell you about … your performance … in those rare edge cases that are not in that data set" (07:19-07:52); and because the run starts from the label, a round-trip check against the original task inputs/outputs gives "the correct labels by construction," so you "basically skip the … expensive ground truth[ing] process" (10:26-11:00). (2) **Build synthetic records coarse to fine by emulating how they were produced**: one-shotting fails because records "are over 300 pages long" and "you wouldn't ask an LLM to write a novel for you in one shot" (03:52-04:31); the layering is patient invariants (sex, birth date, blood group) → an ordered *patient journey* of events and providers in natural language → a document plan per encounter → a fan-out that hydrates each document from the plan plus preceding history (08:10-09:25), chosen because real documentation "is really generated" only during provider encounters (08:58-09:11); the layering keeps "prompt payloads … very token efficient … from both input and output perspective" and scales longer journeys "without overloading the context windows" (09:26-09:50); parallel fan-out forces a refinement loop whose evals include "an LLM based check for consistency between all documents" against "contradictions or inaccuracies or conflicting information" (09:55-10:24); and generation stays in markdown rather than rendering PDFs, since "state of the art PDF parsers" already convert complex PDFs back to markdown, so "all of this synthetic generation [and] evaluation happens in the text domain" (11:04-11:31). (3) **Hand domain experts the pipeline as skills**: "who am I to know what a good medical record looks like? … you really don't. Like no AI engineer would," hence "empower your domain experts to own your whole data pipeline" (11:34-12:07); clinicians steer generation at any point and commonly re-create production-observed cases, modelling failure cases "beforehand or even after … you see them in production" (12:15-12:58); and they own the logic because the pipeline is "a skills-based workflow running on a generic agent harness that we built internally" whose stages (patient journey, document generation, document enrichment, evals) are skills (12:59-13:22), so a clinician adding a customer's intake-form type "could easily just make a new skill file for it, attach it to the pipeline" with no engineering change (13:29-13:46) — generalized as "skills are really an amazing interface between AI engineers and domain experts, especially in vertical AI" (13:47-14:02). Results: synthetic data used "only … for evaluation at the moment," roughly 90% of datasets synthetic, and clinicians distinguishing synthetic from real "about 60% of the time" in blind review (14:09-14:45); datasets "created just in time for these customer deployments," removing the wait for customer data (14:53-15:18). Four takeaways: reverse your inference workflow, sample diversity "from an appropriate distribution for your use case," "emulate the process in which data was actually generated," and give domain experts the keys because they drive "recursive self-improvement and not the AI engineers" (15:24-16:11); scope beyond healthcare — "you don't need a PHI problem for this," it applies whenever data is "ephemeral, sensitive, or even expensive to label" (16:16-16:26). Also strengthened the existing concept production-failure-sets-drive-domain-ai-iteration, from Anterior's own 2025 talk, with this talk's qualification: production cases can be observed but not kept, so they are re-created synthetically, and production samples alone under-cover the tail. Carved against a saturated synthetic-data and domain-expert neighborhood: existing pages own synthetic data for *training* long-tail knowledge into weights, synthetic *queries* calibrated to real user specificity, robotics data pyramids, simulated users validated against humans, and expert *review* tooling and sandboxes — none owned label-first reversal of the task, coarse-to-fine emulation of the generating process, or skills as the authoring handoff to non-engineers. Integrated into Evaluation (synthesis paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + new open question + source), Workflows (pipeline-and-ownership paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + source), Healthcare Operations (fifth-position paragraph reading the two Anterior talks as one argument + 2 Key Concepts + new open question + both sources), and Agents (harness-authorship paragraph + 1 Key Concept + source); concept-index gained 10 grouped entries across Agents, Context Engineering, Evaluation, Healthcare Operations, Tools, and Workflows; index.md Current Emphasis lead and one log.md ingest entry.
