- `YXEqC05WEI0` | [Guardrails First: Engineering Member-Facing Health AI — Rashi Agrawal, Hinge Health](../sources/20260819_YXEqC05WEI0.md) | 2026-08-19 | transcript: `raw/20260819_YXEqC05WEI0/YXEqC05WEI0.en-orig.vtt` | Rashi Agrawal (AI/ML lead, Hinge Health) on guardrails for member-facing health AI. Opens with the production baseline rather than a frontier risk: "40 million people actually use these models for triaging their health care issues," a man hospitalized three weeks with bromide at 200× the safe limit after a chatbot told him to replace salt with sodium bromide, Mount Sinai's first independent safety test of a consumer health AI under-triaging life-threatening emergencies "50% of the times," and ECRI naming AI chatbot misuse the #1 health technology hazard of 2026. Thesis: "most AI safety failures in health care are not model failures. They are architectural decisions that were made before even a single token was generated." Three layers, compressed as "don't policy what you can architect; don't prompt what you can code; don't gate what you can monitor." (1) **PHI is an architecture problem** — stripped at the pipeline boundary at ingestion, "before it ever reaches the data lake," so "a developer opens a dashboard, there's nothing to redact. The PHI was never there"; production and non-production with "no pipes in between"; access scoped by role *and* geographic region; HIPAA/FDA GMLP/state law as "the grounding input," not an overlay — buying "a system that's incapable of certain failures." (2) **Must-not-fail behavior runs in code above the model** — a deterministic layer executes "first, on every turn, before the model even runs," owning 911/988 escalation ("the model should not even see this turn"), high-stakes intent routing (a clinical question silently reaching the tech-support agent is "unrecoverable"), and identity verification ("authentication is a security boundary. Prompts are not"). Justified from the providers' own published authority hierarchy — "root, system, developer, user, guideline… every layer above user is one prompt injection away from being overridden. If the labs themselves don't trust the prompt as a security boundary, neither should you." (3) **Safety is a continuous layer, not a gate** — judges scoring live traffic ("not a saved golden data set") across dozens of always-refreshing dimensions, per-message member thumbs as "the truth signal," sampled traces with 100% coverage on high-stakes cases, and "a new failure that you see in production simply means you now have a new judge," because prompt fixes buy less each round and "the rate never hits zero." Second half is decisioning: five stakeholders (clinical, legal, compliance, product, engineering) see five risks in one issue five days before launch, resolved by five rules — worst plausible outcome sets severity ("a bug that lightly annoys 100% of users is way less severe than one that could cause serious harm in 0.1% of cases"); severity is not capacity, so the only legal moves are fix / delay / accept with explicit sign-off and "you never quietly downgrade a bug"; the default flips by bug class (hold on safety, ship on polish); calibrate to revealed rather than stated risk tolerance ("your revealed risk tolerance is what's actually shipping today"); and humans are the constraint — "judges scale, pattern interpretation doesn't," with fast follows as "committed debt, not an optional backlog." Underneath all five: "in a non-deterministic system, the judge is also non-deterministic. Before you trust the score, verify the scorer" — a clinical-accuracy judge dropping 4.9→4.5 may be over-calling (flagging standard FDA caffeine guidance as hallucination) or correct (the agent said 1,000 mg/day is fine), and "fixing a judge prompt is not cheating. Judges are software, too."
- `Yphdry8ttAQ` | [Trading Desks to Clinical Trials: Parallels in Applied Vertical AI — Ayush Bhardwaj, Allos AI](../sources/20260819_Yphdry8ttAQ.md) | 2026-08-19 | transcript: `raw/20260819_Yphdry8ttAQ/Yphdry8ttAQ.en-orig.vtt` | Ayush Bhardwaj (applied AI at a hedge fund, now Allos AI) abstracts vertical AI into seven steps and locates the failure at step five: the team can build the agent long before it can judge the output, LLM-as-judge "jargons its way out," the reasoning data is withheld by design in finance and pharma so the frontier labs lack it too, and the fix is to hire the user and run a learning loop that starts at error analysis — shipping into an AI-in-the-loop shape where the expert still decides.
- `mav15aW9lLM` | [Why Your Enterprise Tech Stack Isn't Ready for AI Agents — Christopher Lovejoy & Saul Howard](../sources/20260819_mav15aW9lLM.md) | 2026-08-19 | transcript: `raw/20260819_mav15aW9lLM/mav15aW9lLM.en-orig.vtt` | Christopher Lovejoy (forward deployed engineer) and Saul Howard (VP of engineering) of Anterior, "a New York based company selling … agentic AI to US health insurance companies," on why an enterprise AI proof of concept stalls after it succeeds. Framing: healthcare is hard "because of the requirements around process and … compliance" and because of "the direct real impact that your work has on people's lives," but "a lot of the learnings … you can take to enterprise in other regulated industries. Like finance, defense, government work, anywhere where process is so important and has to be followed" (00:50-01:37). The scenario: scope a POC with a large health system, define metrics, two engineers, four weeks; the enterprise stack it must reach spans application layer, control plane, and data plane plus a model provider, with data "across all of these different planes" (01:51-03:12). It works — metrics hit, fast, cheap — and finance asks about next year's budget, the chief medical officer wants to publicize the accuracy, sales asks when "powered by AI" goes on the website, "but the problem is that everyone here is assuming that … the AI was … the challenging part. But actually … often getting things into production is really where the challenge lies" (03:14-03:56). The next day's questions: the audit trail ("every step, every action that the agent takes, every piece of data that it accesses"), sensitive-data handling, clinician escalation and approval, plus prompt injection, ongoing performance, and Epic/Salesforce integrations; four are answered and two explicitly deferred for time (04:00-05:18). Four durable ideas extracted. (1) **An audit trail is a chain of evidence, not a developer log**: "for programmers, an audit trail sounds very like a typical developer log you might have in DataDog," but under "SOC 2, HITRUST, HIPAA … it has to contain a complete record of absolutely every action that the agent took … all of the places where the agent accessed data, all of the authorization by which the agent did something," tested by "say our agent's decisions came up in a court of law. Could we show a justifiable chain of evidence…" (05:23-06:27); the answer is the finance transaction log — "an immutable record of events," append-only, timestamped, complete ("your source of truth for all of the data of the system") and unified ("only one source of truth across all of the different agents that you might have running in parallel") — so "auditability becomes trivial. It falls out of your data storage paradigm" (06:47-07:44); the price is event sourcing's read path ("writes become very easy… reads become more difficult," softened by caching and snapshots but "there always is more effort there"), repaid in healthcare because "all of your views of the data are ephemeral computed projections of the event log," which is what lets a later event change the interpretation of an earlier journey (07:46-08:46). (2) **Object storage beside the log, not inside it**: healthcare data is non-hierarchical, mixed structured/unstructured, over a megabyte per record, RBAC-controlled "both for humans and … agents downstream," and sometimes forbidden to leave the customer's on-prem VPC (09:28-10:32); so schema-driven immutable object storage holds the payload and "the events … only contain references to the schema driven blobs" (10:25-11:03), which lets developers "retrace the agent's steps without having access to the personal health information itself" while still seeing its shape ("because of the schema driven, they can see the shape of that data"), separating "observability and orchestration and instrumentation from the health care data itself" (11:04-11:52); it also gives zero trust a single point — agents "bear tokens and use those tokens to access the data at the point of use and not allow data to flow around the system as it likes" (11:52-12:14) — and turns the lethal trifecta into a solvable constraint: "is it possible within my architecture for the agent to be also accessing data over here?", answered by segregating object storage from the orchestration event stream so "it won't be possible for the agent to access data within the same process" (12:15-12:56). (3) **Treat humans and models as the same kind of agent**: escalation is unpredictable (model-initiated when unsure, or rule-triggered above a treatment threshold) and "humans and LLMs ultimately process context differently" (12:57-13:47), so widen the platform's definition of agent — "any action that can be taken by an LLM could also be taken by a human," the escalation can happen anywhere in the chain, and "any step downstream doesn't care about whether it was a human or an LLM that did those actions upstream" (13:47-14:16), with one shared context definition mapped "into something that's agent friendly, like a prompt, or into something that's more human friendly, for example, a UI" (14:19-14:41). (4) **Evals emerge from the primitives**: against non-determinism, unrepresentative offline samples, and drift (14:46-15:32), the three primitives "give you effective privacy preserving evals almost as a byproduct" — ledger replay to "see the complete state of the system at that point in time" and tweak one prompt/model/code path for "the exact direct impact" (15:52-16:15), human-agent equivalency so that for any task "your difference is your eval" (16:16-16:29), and object storage to "run these evals on production data including inside your customer's environment without actually ever exposing that data" (16:30-16:46). Also strengthened the existing concept sequence-production-ai-by-pillars-and-choose-the-model-last with the architectural version of its thesis: architecture is "taking your constraints very seriously and thinking about what you want to be simple within the system and then choosing the trade-offs" (06:29-06:45, 17:16-17:37); "there are patterns that have worked very well within finance, within defense, within big tech" (17:37-17:56); failure is "strapping on the enterprise requirements as you come across them … you end up with something very brittle, something very hard to … externalize and to generalize," success is treating the constraints as "the architectural principles that you're going to build everything upon and then build back up towards that POC accuracy using your new primitives" (17:56-18:52). Carved against a saturated observability/replay neighborhood by owning framings no page held: existing pages own developer-facing node-boundary recording and replay-as-test-case, immutable versioned state for handoffs, agents-as-first-class-users with audit trails, and strip-PHI-at-ingestion — while these own the *regulatory* definition of an audit trail plus the ledger that satisfies it, the payload/event split as a security and debuggability boundary, human-agent substitutability as a platform primitive rather than an approval gate, and evals as a byproduct of compliance architecture. Integrated into Healthcare Operations (seventh-position two-paragraph synthesis reading the equivalency primitive against the cluster's five other human-in-the-loop arrangements + 4 Key Concepts + 2 open questions + source), Infrastructure (data-plane paragraph + 3 Key Concepts + open question + source), Security (paragraph + 2 Key Concepts + open question + source), Agents (escalation-primitive paragraph + 3 Key Concepts + open question + source), and Evaluation (byproduct paragraph + 1 Key Concept + open question + source); concept-index gained 13 grouped entries across Agents, Infrastructure, Healthcare Operations, Evaluation, and Security; index.md Current Emphasis lead and one 2026-08-19 log.md ingest entry. - `u6q-byPWUuo` | [From Ambient Documentation to Clinical Intelligence — Chaitanya Asawa, Abridge](../sources/20260819_u6q-byPWUuo.md) | 2026-08-19 | transcript: `raw/20260819_u6q-byPWUuo/u6q-byPWUuo.en-orig.vtt` | Chaitanya Asawa, who leads Abridge's engineering for clinical decision support and agentic experiences, on moving from ambient clinical documentation into products downstream of the same doctor-patient conversation. Wedge and thesis: the note after every visit costs "2 hours a day" of after-work "pajama time" and is high-stakes both ways (basis of billing; the longitudinal context the next clinician inherits), which got the documentation product into "300 of the largest health systems in the United States, Kaiser, Mayo, Johns Hopkins, Sutter" in 2-3 years; from there "everything is around the conversation… billing, clinical trial matching, clinical decision support," with pre-visit discussion topics, post-visit summary/note/pended orders, and context drawn from the EHR, the live conversation, and medical literature plus guidelines. Framing: three KPIs (quality, latency, cost) "on hard mode," contrasted with Glean where "I could be wrong and it would have been fine." **Quality** — "we really treat evals as the operating system": internal benchmarks before any product, staged rollout (trusted alpha clinicians → beta → A/B at scale) because "not everything offline will perfectly represent what happens in practice," continual monitoring after, plus online signals (note edits, thumbs, stars, free text); clinicians embedded company-wide, and "how can we, the rest of the company, still move fast is by encoding that clinician judgement into LM judges." **The eval crux** — for contextual clinical decision support "the generator and verifier gap is really small. If I had a really really good generator verifier, then that would just be my generator itself" (contrast: Sudoku is hard to generate, trivial to verify), answered by many partial signals (clinical quality, boundary/adversarial, clinical safety, tone/style judges) and by manufacturing the reference: real cases → two physicians independently write rubrics of required response *elements* (no golden answer, since "there's many infinite possible responses") → a third adjudicates them into one → a fourth QAs → an LLM judge semantic-matches. **Cost/latency at ~100M medical conversations a year** — decompose the note into its sections (HPI, PMH, assessment and plan) and post-train small models per section since "health care is actually many specific workflows" and "we don't need frontier level intelligence for every problem"; train your own only with a "right to win" (quality already maxed out → train for cost/latency; not maxed → only if unique data plus a problem the labs ignore can "beat the rate of change on the frontier model"), backed by a dataset of ~100M conversations/year that "as far as we know, no one else has"; and capture in-visit spoken orders via "a number of different gates that are cheaper and faster" that trigger heavier order-matching models, because polling "every few seconds … would really break the bank."
- `yoONZwV2smc` | [AI is the World's largest Relationship Therapist — Clay Cockrell & Tony Fabrikant, CoupleWork AI](../sources/20260819_yoONZwV2smc.md) | 2026-08-19 | transcript: `raw/20260819_yoONZwV2smc/yoONZwV2smc.en-orig.vtt` | Clay Cockrell (couples counselor of 30 years, co-founder of CoupleWork, "an AI relationship coaching platform") and co-founder Tony Fabrikant on consumer AI as the world's largest relationship therapist. Scale argument: BetterHelp assembled ~35,000 licensed clinicians and 5M users over a decade; ChatGPT sees "approximately 900 million active users per week," with "a substantial percentage… engaging with AI for emotional support and relationship guidance… just because it's there. Because it's 11:00 p.m. and they just had an argument" — stated with its evidence limit ("we do not yet have measurement of the scale, but we have enough signal"). Thesis: "AI is going to be the most transformative force in human relationship history. And most of what is being built right now is going to make it worse," and the diagnosis is "insufficient domain expertise being applied to one of the highest stakes domains in human life." (1) **Agreeableness as the failure mode**: the model is "optimized for one thing above all else, keeping you engaged" — more sessions, longer sessions, "more emotional reliance on the AI" — which is "amazing" on a SaaS dashboard and "a fire alarm" clinically; the demo reply ("That sounds really invalidating. Your feelings are completely valid…") is "a very expensive mirror that only shows you in your best light," and across sessions "the person does not become more self-aware. They become more certain. They come back to the relationship in the shape of an attorney with cleaner narrative, stronger case, and less curiosity about what their partner was experiencing"; "a helpful, agreeable, fast couples therapist is basically a bartender with better vocabulary." Counter-metric: the user should "return to the relationship better than they came in, more regulated, more honest, less defended, less reactive," since "my job is to work myself out of a job," and the anti-goal is "a beautifully designed avoidance loop." (2) **Encode the validated method**: Gottman's 40-year love lab identified patterns predicting divorce "with over 90% accuracy… from a 15-minute conversation," and EFT (Sue Johnson, attachment theory — "when couples fight about the dishes, it's never about the dishes") has outcome research "among the strongest in psychotherapy"; both take years of training and are "almost entirely absent from the commercial AI relationship space," which runs on "general empathy plus communication tips." Their coach Maxine's "response logic is grounded in 40 years of couples research": "she does not simply validate. She reflects patterns back. She names dynamics, not just feelings. She asks the question underneath the question." (3) **Background screening for the wrong category of help**: a general assistant cannot hear "the difference between we fight a lot and I'm afraid of what happens when I disagree with him," nor that "I just have to stay calm is sometimes the exact sentence someone is taught to say right before something terrible happens," and relationship coaching "sits closer to suicide risk and yes, homicide risk than almost any other corner of mental health"; so before responding to a sensitive message Maxine screens "quietly in the background" for "escalating control, fear-based language, anything pointing toward risk to the user or someone else," then "stops coaching and starts following the protocols" — built "out of 30 years of clinical practice, not a generic safety policy written by someone who's never sat in the room." "Knowing when not to coach is just as important as to know how." (4) **Privilege as the data bar**: first-session disclosures include "the fight from an hour ago… financial secrets, affairs, mental health history, sometimes your kids' names," which default into "training pipelines, server logs, and product analytics built by teams optimizing for engagement, not confidentiality" and "the same data infrastructure as your search history and your shopping cart"; consumer AI has no equivalent of clinical privilege, so the adopted bar is "not is this legally defensible, the bar is would I be comfortable if this were my own marriage on the line." (5) **Fabrikant's eval close**: "start with your clinician, not with your prompt… encode what good looks like in evals. Write hundreds of evals TDD style"; "watch those evals fail. Run your agent through them thousands of times, tens of thousands of times. Watch for those outliers. When safety's on the line, even one failing test is not okay"; then "nothing replaces your gut when emotional context is on the line. So eat your own dog food… Feel when the tone starts shifting off," dogfooded literally with his own partner. Closing rule: "partner with a clinician who challenges you… so that you can create AI that challenges your users." Positioning claim for the category: "a majority of couples wait 6 years before seeking professional help," so "this is not a consumer app opportunity. This is a public health opportunity hiding inside consumer behavior."
- `-tviRdpmHvs` | [Training Krea 2: What matters in generative model training — Sangwu Lee, Krea.ai](../sources/20260818_-tviRdpmHvs.md) | 2026-08-18 | transcript: `raw/20260818_-tviRdpmHvs/-tviRdpmHvs.en-orig.vtt` | Sangwu Lee on how Krea trained its Krea 2 image foundation model, whose medium variant is open-sourced, from the research rather than infrastructure side. Positioning: the big-lab production models ("Chat GPT-2," Nano Banana Pro) optimize for slow, near-flawless output, taking "up to like a minute or like two," and pay for it with distribution collapse — "in order to get like good consistency, they have like kind of like significantly mode collapse their models," because "the easiest and most reliable way to like render a person is render the most boring average person that exists and then like put it in a center frame," so "burning skull" returns competent, near-identical images. That is right for "a poster or like a birthday card" and wrong for "when you're like a creative studio, you don't quite know what you want yet," which is the segment Krea targets with fast generation, controls, and stylistic diversity. Architecture is a latent diffusion transformer (autoencoder compression because DiTs are quadratic, so "if you try to like model every single pixel, that's very expensive"). **Thesis**: "you lock in your architecture and then… a lot of work just goes into like just feeding the model like what it wants… I cannot iterate this more"; "data is like what really determines the quality of your model"; ~30-40 in-house classifiers, heuristics, and filters. **Curation specifics** — a diversity goal forbids generic quality filtering ("some people like think… low-resolution CRT videos are like a bad image, but some people like that kind of like aesthetics… don't just rely on like very standard like aesthetic scores"); AI-generated images are removed entirely because distillation "does like provide you a shortcut" but "synthetic data is like so sticky… you're going to get like very like ChatGPT or like Nano [Banana] aesthetic," which he can detect by eye; the captioning pipeline runs OCR first "because text rendering is quite important," then optional metadata, then a VLM detail pass, then rewriting into JSON or other prompt formats; images whose captions consistently omit a visible property get filtered or undersampled, the example being a painting photographed framed on a white wall that the captioner never mentions, so "when you try to generate a painting of whatever, it'll be always hanged on a wall, on a white wall"; deduplication is cost-ordered over "2 to like 10 billion images" (pHash/MD5 first, SSCD/SigLIP embeddings on the reduced set) and filters are built by prompting a large VLM ("does this look like a AI image or not") then distilling to "a very small like SigLip classifier," since "if you run a classifier over like a billion images, you do need things to be like SigLip-sized" — the same move Essential Web used on text with a ~500M-parameter student; sparse autoencoders trained on vision models act as "a unsupervised tagging system" whose activated features (horse, black and white, blurry) become filter or oversampling criteria for "signatures or like watermarks or like some kind of like border artifacts"; and world-knowledge coverage is checked with the original CLIP paper's trick — PageRank over every Wikipedia concept, top percentile as keywords, plain-text or embedding search against the dataset — reported honestly as "frankly, I don't know how much this has helped." **Training pipeline**, described as LLM-inspired: 256→1K resolution curriculum (low res learns "how a horse looks like," high res learns structure and detail), mid-training and SFT on curated illustration/graphic design/photography/cinematics "to kind of mold your distribution" since a pre-trained diffusion model is "just basically auto complete," preference optimization on A/B pairs where "we get a little bit more opinionated," GRPO-inspired RL sending generations "to the reward servers" to fix text rendering and anatomy, and a trained prompt expander that is "almost essential… for production grade diffusion models" because long detailed prompts "are more in distribution with your model's… training data." In progress: multi-expert on-policy distillation merging capability-specialized experts into one student. **What mattered**: fast-iteration infrastructure; data, because "methods can change… but data is like eternal"; low-hyperparameter methods; efficiency; and stealing from LLM research "so that I can just reuse their kernels." Closing observation: with an autoregressive prompt expander feeding a diffusion encoder the stack is inverting back toward DALL-E 2's shape, and he wants to drop VAEs and text encoders for "a single clean transformer" while exploring richer conditioning now that VLMs cheaply produce bounding boxes (Ideogram) or scene graphs (Fei-Fei Li's lab, 2017) — "image generation… is really like a proxy for… VLM… progress."
- `5dCAmSDOAjI` | [The Next Medium: Why Real-Time Interactive Video Changes Everything — Ahmed Ahres, Reactor](../sources/20260818_5dCAmSDOAjI.md) | 2026-08-18 | transcript: `raw/20260818_5dCAmSDOAjI/5dCAmSDOAjI.en-orig.vtt` | Ahmed Ahres (Reactor) argues real-time generation is a change of medium rather than a speedup (maps to GPS, film to viewfinder), narrows "world model" to infinite/interactive/steerable video, splits the field into prompted scenes, controllable-character worlds, and not-yet-working live avatars, and lands the engineering consequence: streaming pixels instead of returned files, stateful sessions whose memory must be maintained, sub-100 ms latency forcing GPUs near users, and consistency evaluation that nobody, including DeepMind, has solved.
- `E_Txocq-Lrw` | [While my guitar gently speaks — Todd Fisher, Philo Ventures](../sources/20260818_E_Txocq-Lrw.md) | 2026-08-18 | transcript: `raw/20260818_E_Txocq-Lrw/E_Txocq-Lrw.en-orig.vtt` | Todd Fisher on a personal project that makes an electric guitar speak and sing — a complete small AI product whose model layer is entirely commodity and whose every hard problem sits in the surrounding signal path. Lineage framing: pickup into a speaker, stomp boxes, Peter Frampton's talk box ("the sound of the guitar… through an actual hose… put it in my mouth"), then software emulation in Pro Tools/Logic/Fruity Loops, asking "what is that next evolution of the guitar… with AI in the picture?" **Delivery**: built with **JUCE** — "really awesome for anyone building audio software" — as a plugin loaded into Logic Pro, "just like any other plugin in Logic where you just plop it in there… chaining all the effects together," on the premise that "your DAW is effectively your IDE but for musicians and music producers." **v1**: typed text → **Piper** or "built-in Apple" TTS → clip played on a note event (the demo word is "Developers"). **v2, the hard part**: slicing the clip per word, because "in English or in any languages for that matter, there's more than one word." Energy-gap segmentation ("there's typically silence in between words… let's just cut it whenever the decibels are very much close to zero") fails on running speech — "as I'm speaking right now… there's actually no silence in between some of my words… not 100% foolproof"; a sonority-peak syllabifier ("identifying the syllables… there's vowels in here. Vowels typically lead to syllables") is added to it; the combination is "working pretty well. Not quite as good as I want to," so the shipped answer is manual: "I could drag this and manually edit some of these segments." **Singing v1**: **YIN** pitch detection to recover the fundamental from a fretted note, an ADSR-shaped synthesized sawtooth ("the levers to figure out how to actually make the note sound in different ways"), pushed through the voice clip (the talk-box analogy, "filling up the cavity of the voice") and a **vocoder**, with a "clarity" lever "balance or mixing the synthesized note with the actual voice that the AI is giving." **Conversational**: mic → **Whisper** → "run a local model on my computer. It doesn't really matter what LLM, just any local model" → output "plop it on the guitar"; an audience question ("What is reality?") is answered word by word — "a choppy version, but it is working. So, that's a win." **Singing v2**: open-source **VocalSet** singing recordings pitch-shifted with the **WORLD** library and mapped per fret, but "this is a very heavy process, and so I can't really do that live. I have to effectively pre-bake that," so only the five vowel sounds were baked — "not quite your opera singer, but getting closer." Two live demos fail on stage ("Live demos always the best"), and he declines to play the song the title alludes to because the recording was going online. Closing charge: build the passion project, "because nowadays with AI, we could build so many really cool things, and time is typically not the big time suck that it once was."
- `VBCDhRrvlYo` | [The Next Game Engine Won't Have a Manual — Arturo Nunez, Nereu](../sources/20260818_VBCDhRrvlYo.md) | 2026-08-18 | transcript: `raw/20260818_VBCDhRrvlYo/VBCDhRrvlYo.en-orig.vtt` | Arturo Nunez, almost ten years at Unity before building Nereu, on why a coding agent pointed at a conventional game engine reinvents the same solution every time and what layer fixes it. **Diagnosis**: ask for "a camera that follows this character" and "the LLM reinvents the wheel every single time. Well, when the result is going to be essentially the same," because engines plus agents are "just building a bridge between two worlds… you still need to know kind of like what to ask in vocabulary of an engine, in the vocabulary of code. Otherwise… the LLM goes rogue." Stated as a layering error: "by default, the context is on the game engine rather than on the game design part, and I think we should flip that idea." The boilerplate that vocabulary forces — to control a character you need a mesh, "a renderer, an animator, a rigid body, and collider for physics, an audio source, and then you add your movement logic," almost all of it shared by "every single character in every single game out there," with "hundreds of sliders" to read. **Inversion**: "everything is just an asset… and you just add tags to describe the intent of those assets," the vocabulary taken from how games are taught ("the tutorial tells you, 'Oh, press A to jump and press A again while you're in the air to do a double jump'"), plus event rules ("when you collect a coin, I want you to increase the score"). The mechanism is ATS (captioned "asset tax system"), Entity Component System / data-oriented design applied to the agent: "there are systems that query for all the assets in the world and say… I'm going to move all the objects that have the vehicle, the player, and the drivable tag," so "nothing prevents you from adding the vehicle and drivable tag to your building and then you have a building that you can put in a Mario Kart style of game." The agent's whole action space is tags — "the agent just performs calls and appends or removes the tags. All these tags and all these systems are built into the engine" — and "we don't have a scripting system in there. That's on purpose," though the engine "is just JavaScript and runs on the browser" for anyone who wants to extend it. **Context assembly**, the most transferable engineering detail: feeding the whole scene blows up context ("I had like 100 assets… most of them are like grass that we could ignore"), so he borrows rendering's level of detail — near objects get "a higher quality texture… material… model" while distant ones become a cube nobody notices — and applies it to the prompt: assets around the user's current selection arrive "information about the tags that they have and the values of the settings on those tags," mid-distance ones collapse to "there's something that's a player here at this position, but I'm not going to send you the entire context," and the grading updates "as a user keeps moving around." Prompt inputs also include the asset *types* present ("if you're using robots, if you're using something like medieval") and a user-written description of the game. **Asset catalog**: "six or 7,000 assets. I could not manually tag them all… I just have the names and the 3D file. So, I took a screenshot and ran a vision model to describe those," while the live assistant is "mostly an LLM." **Build process**: discuss with Claude Code (captioned "Cloud Code"), consult specialist friends per area (e.g. lighting), delete settings and features users do not touch "in order to simplify the engine," and hand each new tool definition to the assistant as it ships; currently a closed alpha. **Where the difficulty moves**: "the challenging part is the composing games into these tags and into these systems… there are things like the mood of the game. Like I want to make a platformer but that feels like scary. Well, that also touches on… post-processing effects and… lighting." **Product position**: the assistant "essentially helps you get unstuck," not one-shot generation — "I don't want us to one-shot games that nobody is going to play," conceding the industry has that need — with the intended residue being that users "learn along the way the language of making games, the language of game design, not necessarily the coding or programming." **On world models**: "that's going to be a different medium even if we call them video games," bounded by the incumbent bar — games "have to render 60 frames per second. And doing that at 4K resolutions in real time with a world model, I think it's still far away," plus simulated physics.
- `Xln-On3syJk` | [Generative Video at the Speed of Light — Keegan McCallum, uRun](../sources/20260818_Xln-On3syJk.md) | 2026-08-18 | transcript: `raw/20260818_Xln-On3syJk/Xln-On3syJk.en-orig.vtt` | Keegan McCallum, founder of uRun ("a new kind of inference provider focused around interactive media"), on why the interesting axis in generative video stopped being quality. **The two axes**: quality has a visible lineage — "the classic Will Smith eating spaghetti from 2023… nightmare fuel," "in 2024, we got Sora and it gets a little better," Sora 2 "even better," and Seedance "this year… absolutely incredible. So photorealistic" — but the one he tracks is "efficiency and… the long horizon generations." **The parity claim**: real-time clips "generated faster than you can consume them… are about at the same quality as the frontier models were last year," and in a side-by-side the minutes-long generation is "arguably a bit better. It's got better motion" while costing on the order of a hundred times more (the caption sentence attaches "about a 100th of the cost" ambiguously; the index description states the direction). **The model**: "Helios is a distill of [Wan] 2.1 14B," out in March, and "these are techniques that are being applied all over the place, not just the one model," with "at least 40 models with real-time capabilities and long horizon generation capabilities released this year" across world models ("keep consistency over long horizons and you can control in a fine-grained way, the camera and the viewport"), avatar models ("like we just talked about with LemonSlice"), and video-to-video ("transform what you're seeing in real time, almost like a magic mirror"). **The economics**: after a show of hands for burning "10 or even $50 worth of tokens in an hour" on coding, "$10 can get you 3 hours worth of generated video continuously with most of these models, and $50 would give you an entire day interacting with an AI in a visual medium. 15 hours." **What that unlocks**: a webcam magic mirror ("ask to see yourself in any outfit… in a car you like or with a haircut you're considering"); an accessibility argument — "working with AI involves a lot of reading and a lot of text. For some people that's more difficult. For some people they just don't think in text. They think visually and learn better that way," aiming at "companions or visual mediums that are going to allow more people to experience the things a lot of us have with coding models"; and content creation that stops being "a slot machine type approach where you're setting up a prompt and maybe some keyframes and spending about $10 a minute to try and get the shot that you want," replaced by steering "in real time in under a second while they're generating," plus "piloting an agent that you're able to look over its shoulder and see what it's generating in real time," then rendering "a more full fidelity clip" with a stronger model. **The serving thesis**, and the technical core: "GPUs all over the world potentially if you've got a global audience… where you're connecting the users to, what GPUs you're going to use to serve them… probably WebRTC and ICE and TURN. And for the most interesting use cases, you're going to want to wire multiple models together in continuous streaming workflows. Building those real-time harnesses, and you're going to want things synchronized with your controls that you're providing to your end users with every frame." **Packaging**: "a React component that you could drop into your application… with any model" over "a programmable Python runtime that lets you easily build these complex pipelines generating asynchronously," plus "a CLI or an MCP server" because "in 2026, [we] don't just need platforms, we need software factories and ways for agents [to] interact with these." **Closing**: "the models are here and the frontier is really in how we serve them."
- `byn9PURoBNY` | [Infra behind Krea 2: How to train and serve at scale — Gabriel Jorge Menezes, Krea.ai](../sources/20260818_byn9PURoBNY.md) | 2026-08-18 | transcript: `raw/20260818_byn9PURoBNY/byn9PURoBNY.en-orig.vtt` | Gabriel Jorge Menezes on the infrastructure half of Krea 2, the image model pre-trained from scratch on thousands of InfiniBand-connected GPUs whose research half Sangwu Lee covered separately the same day. **Observability**: "metrics are everything… don't go blind because you're going to go crazy," and the defaults are wrong — "there is GPU utilization which is a lie… amount of time GPU's doing work but like not good work," reading 100% through a pre-training run that was nowhere near well used, so tensor core utilization is the proxy, validated by climbing as resolution stepped 128 → 256 → 512 → 1024 pixels across pre-, mid-, and post-training. The counters that mattered most are unexported: "most of our failures were like related to like cross-node communication," but NVIDIA's DCGM exports no InfiniBand metrics and no NVLink error counters, so they built collection themselves ("It was not hard"), tracking fabric message wait time, typed error counts, and packet counts; a node whose GPUs "seem to be fine" is diagnosed through NVLink errors and replaced. Verdict: "without this we would not be able to to train at all." **Failure posture**: crashes scale with cluster size and many are silent ("NCCL timeouts… and the metrics are all good"); after being "paranoid, swap node, change node," they learned "sometimes you just let it crash" because the same machines, same code, and same data run an hour, crash, then go 12-24 hours. A Meta paper (unnamed) gives an expected-failure baseline they matched in pattern but not in numbers — their runs lasted far less, and "pre-training on runs that last less than 8 hours, it is a problem." Hardware is replaced on a threshold, not debugged: "if there is any GPUs above like 78°, you remove them. Don't try to be smart." The absorber is checkpointing — "use and abuse the file system" — at 20-30 minute intervals writing ~1 TB in under 30 seconds against a cluster doing ~1.8 TB/s reads and ~1 TB/s writes; Ceph came first, "broke," and "we lost trust in data," hence "if you have the money go with something paid." **Scheduling**: researchers submit and never think about GPUs; an open-source Kubernetes project (captioned indistinguishably from "queue"; the described gang scheduling, workload-priority tier above pod priority, and manually specified per-queue quotas match Kueue) places training jobs, and its rough edge is quota drift as "nodes phasing in and out of existence… sometimes these would break gang scheduling." Training outranks production outright — "if there's inference running on those machines, the inference gets kicked out" — justified by marginal value: "the value that we get off the GPUs doing trainings is more… higher than… we get out of production." **Eviction mechanism**: virtual-kubelet creates "a fake machine in Kubernetes" so pods that do not fit locally schedule onto external providers through a provider adapter with a selection algorithm and two-sided reconciliation; failures need no custom handling ("mark the pod as failed. Kubernetes… create[s] a new one… You don't need to try to save the world"); a Prometheus-driven taint on the fake node keeps pods local while GPUs are free and lifts when a training run hogs them; and a descheduler migrates pods back slowly afterward, explicitly not a NoExecute taint, which "would kick everything out at the same time… Production would go down." Result: "a very self-healing system… changed the way we do research cuz no one else needs to care about GPUs." **Closing asymmetry**: diffusion transformers "are not huge like LLMs that need like multi-node inference," so "whatever GPU works. The GPU can be hot, falling out of the bus. It can be exploding. Inference still going to run." Krea 2 ships two open checkpoints (raw pre-trained for post-training, and a sub-second "turbo") and serves production on krea.ai.
- `pPj_tjlvYjA` | [Building an Agentic Video Editor for Mass Consumer — Ekaterina Deyneka, Reelful](../sources/20260818_pPj_tjlvYjA.md) | 2026-08-18 | transcript: `raw/20260818_pPj_tjlvYjA/pPj_tjlvYjA.en-orig.vtt` | Ekaterina Deyneka (founder/CEO, Reelful) opens on a behavioral gap — nearly every hand in the room had recorded video at the conference, almost none had posted any of it, "because video editing is hard… it's tedious and it's largely still manual." Reelful's loop: drop in photos and videos plus context or directions ("add captions, add music, add voiceover"), and the agent "understand[s] your media, find[s] the right moments, assemble[s] everything together, generate[s] captions, music, voiceover, b-rolls"; a second framing case is a speak-to-camera take with pauses and unsuccessful shots the agent removes. Three new concepts. (1) reuse-the-agentic-app-builder-shape-for-non-code-artifacts — "from the infrastructure standpoint, agentic video editor is very similar to agentic app builder": prompt UI (media plus prompt) → "a remote machine which is called sandbox… spinning up, and inside this machine, there is an agent with tools and skills" → artifact (codebase vs video composition) → output (app preview vs rendered video). The swap is licensed by the artifact format — Remotion, "an open-source framework to create videos as code, as React code… just like a file with the order with all your assets and tracks," chosen because "agents are really good at writing code" — which also supplies a mechanical failure mode, so a verification layer checks the composition "is clean, is well defined, everything will be rendered" and "if there are some problems then the agent will reiterate." Domain taste lives in skills: cut rules for selecting best moments, font pairs, b-roll generation — "this is where our taste and craft live"; the agent also spawns sub-processes for fitted music, voiceover, sounds, and photo animation. (2) editing-real-material-is-harder-for-agents-than-generating — Reelful does "not generate a lot of content" and expects "your real life, your personal content"; "if the agent has a blank canvas, it can do whatever they can. But in the editing case, the agent has to figure out which moments are the best… what to omit," while footage "can be messy or incomplete" and the bar is absolute: "ideally the viewers of this content don't get if it is like AI or human edited." (3) package-agentic-pipelines-behind-templates-and-a-manual-editor — delivery is "even maybe a bigger problem" than the pipeline: mobile-first (edit "while driving, walking or maybe lifting weights"), directional templates because "prompting videos can sometimes be also challenging" so "even without any prompt it will work," a creative plan approved before the sandbox starts, and a conventional editor afterward for tweaks like "remove a second or maybe correct some word in the captions." Also corroborates match-agent-output-medium-to-its-native-representation (Remotion as an independent second instance, in video) and author-visual-artifacts-as-html-decoupled-from-delivery-format (composition = editing format, MP4 = delivery format). Early product, second version released at the conference, recently funded by a16z speedrun.
- `z1dqv74SpUs` | [Voice agents with Realtime Video — Sidney Primas, LemonSlice](../sources/20260818_z1dqv74SpUs.md) | 2026-08-18 | transcript: `raw/20260818_z1dqv74SpUs/z1dqv74SpUs.en-orig.vtt` | Sidney Primas (CTO/founder, LemonSlice) on building a real-time photorealistic avatar, the third and least-mature class in the wiki's real-time-video taxonomy. **Mission and evidence**: "break the Avatar Turing test" — an avatar "indistinguishable from a human on a video call" — with a Microsoft deployment putting Teddy Roosevelt in a replica Oval Office inside his presidential library, "definitely not Turing test solved yet, but like a big milestone"; full body, hands, actions, movement, all real time. **Why a visual layer**: humans "struggle to focus and pay attention… even voice it's hard to really lock in," so the bet is "most interactions between AI and humans will have a visual layer." **Architecture bet**: "we take these world models and we focus them on humans… harder to get the initial model working… harder to train… harder to deploy," in exchange for emergent full body movement, object interactions, scene movement, micro-expressions and emotions that "don't come for free, but come more for free than… the other approaches"; deflated as "basically a video model that understands the physics of the world." Demo evidence: any single image (photorealistic, Pixar, cartoon), clothes and scene changed "within the same video call and on the same kind of inference setup," "physics on the earrings… The water moves." **Commercial shape**: "we're mostly the API layer… People bring their own LLM. People bring their own usually like voices. And then we're the visual layer on top of it," so "anything today where you have a voice agent, you now also kind of a video agent." **Training**: their own video DiT; "the audio turns out to be very important for getting emotions right and the facial expressions right," and "most audio encoders today are trained on basically audiobooks, which is very monotone… if you want to have a very expressive model, you can't use those audio encoders." **Interactive**: batch video models "are bidirectional… can look into the future," so they "train a model with an attention mask so that the model can only look into the past… it doesn't see audio in the future." **Real time**: "let's say 30 steps" of denoising "bring it out to one step." **Error accumulation**: "each video block you generate has some error in it… you're adding more error to it, and then the error compounds over time," against a horizon of "8 hours straight with like no reset" (a sixteen-hour deployment planned); an undisclosed fix is claimed. **Cost**: "the costs are about the same as a voice model. Which is crazy to me… it's much more pixel heavy in a video model," restated in Q&A as "the same level as an audio model in terms of what we charge for it," enabling consumer customers; wants it cheaper for higher resolution, expects "algorithm improvements and hardware improvements" plus a shift "to more of a token approach instead of a diffusion approach." **Model harness** (captioned "hardness"): "often overlooked but… actually super important and super hard… a lot of our value actually in productizing this is in the model harness" — separate threads, GPU and CPU work, "orchestrate this perfectly in a way that the video always remains real time. There is never any stutter," "especially hard when you have things like interrupts, you have queues, you're buffering data, you have to clean the queues"; expects "a lot more of the value… will be in figuring out the model harness… especially true for any real-time applications." **Next**: an emotion engine predicting the right action "based on the audio input from the avatar and the text input for what the avatar is going to say," 1-2 months out, motivated by "our model can already do this today. It has the capabilities… It's just not controllable enough… and not deterministic enough to be useful with the conversation." **Long-term**: "a single model that is the EQ layer for AI" taking user video and audio in and avatar video and audio out, modeling "its own internal emotional state," while "a separate model… will drive the IQ… the tool calling… the deep thinking"; 2-3 years. **Internal state**: today "basically words. If you can describe it in words, you can generate those actions and emotions"; later a latent state, "not human interpretable necessarily." **Eval**: "our own version of the Turing test for these avatars, which will just include real people… we won't pass it this year," built to track over time and be published.
- `MkRYPFIMCSA` | [Security Firewall for Agents — Ryan Dahl, Deno](../sources/20260817_MkRYPFIMCSA.md) | 2026-08-17 | transcript: `raw/20260817_MkRYPFIMCSA/MkRYPFIMCSA.en-orig.vtt` | Ryan Dahl (CEO, Deno; creator of Node.js) reports a live deployment — incident-response agents (OpenClaw named, "but other agents as well") with read **and write** access to production Postgres, Kubernetes, ClickHouse, AWS, GitHub, and Slack against a real pager rotation, "able to solve quite a lot of incidences where we previously would have a human [SRE] in the loop" — and then argues where the security boundary for that deployment has to sit. **The rejected control**: he grants the model behaves ("Opus is remarkably well aligned… it will refuse over and over again") and declines it anyway — "security can't just be wishful thinking that Opus will always obey your wishes" — because the agents are wired into the support system and so "can be prompt injected from the outside." The stance: "the agents themselves have to be untrusted software. You can't put the guard inside the agent," explicitly including "security plugins or modifications to the agent software itself." Q&A extends it forward: better models make it "less and less of a problem," but "we're always going to have to have backstop security mechanisms." **The reframing**: agents run on standalone VMs, so filesystem and syscall sandboxes are beside the point, and "every nefarious action… every good action… comes in the form of some network communication, some bytes over the wire" — formed via MCP "but also subprocesses," including non-HTTP protocols the agent can spawn (`psql`). **The hard case**: production Postgres inside a VPC reachable only through an EKS endpoint, where the agent must not "tunnel through this EKS server, spawn psql, and drop the users table" — summarized as "an outbound path the agent's host can't reach, on a protocol that isn't HTTP, that's gated by a rule that understands SQL." **Why the alternatives fail**: read-only credentials and ACLs are "true up to a point" but require careful provisioning across many systems and "the composition of access can lead to holes when you can access one system and then another system"; carefully permissioned MCP tools work until "as soon as [the agent] spawns the psql, you're broken through the security boundary"; LLM gateways with guardrails (OpenRouter, LiteLLM) see only the provider leg; HTTP proxies (httpjail, Brex's Crabtrap with an LLM judge) sit at the HTTP layer; OS process sandboxes guard a different axis. **The system (Claw Patrol, open-source MIT)**: a proxy in front of the agent operating "not at the HTTP level but at a lower level," understanding "each and every byte flowing out," with rules that apply "even when tunneling through other systems" and a plugin system for unknown protocols; it holds credentials (cookies, Postgres, ClickHouse, OAuth, AWS SigV4) so the agent "doesn't ever actually see secret values"; it treats the agent as a black box requiring "no changes to that software"; rule outcomes can reject, ask an LLM judge, ask a human in a Slack channel, "or any combination thereof"; the enforcement unit is an "action… because it's more general than HTTP requests." **Policy as code**: rules in HCL (the Terraform configuration language), checked into git, ~1,000 lines at Deno with "each and every change" reviewed, and a fixture-request test system so "you can essentially create unit tests to make sure that… request will always be blocked." **Deployment**: over Tailscale or WireGuard, agents in a tailnet with Claw Patrol as the exit node, Tailscale identity reused for dashboard auth, predicated on "all of our stuff is off the internet." **Volunteered caveat**: "Claw Patrol itself is holding all of these credentials to production systems. So you have to be very careful with it." **Demo**: Codex in yolo mode obeys "delete the users table," spawns psql, and the proxy rejects the action at the Postgres wire protocol.
- `WP3hjUXd918` | [Context Engineering in 2026 — Louis-François Bouchard, Omar Solano & Samridhi Vaid, Towards AI](../sources/20260817_WP3hjUXd918.md) | 2026-08-17 | transcript: `raw/20260817_WP3hjUXd918/WP3hjUXd918.en-orig.vtt` | A three-part Towards AI workshop that surveys the 2026 compaction toolkit and then measures it against their own open-source AI tutor, with most of the toolkit losing. **The measurement**: 11 presets (full history, their shipped production defaults, and six techniques including sliding window, prompt compression, and selective retention) with model, prompt, tools, and dataset held fixed, run on two task shapes — 60 real student Q&A pairs scraped by Codex, and multi-turn sessions with a planted fact, filler, and a probe — graded by code check plus an LLM judge, gated on whether the technique under test actually fired, at a cost of almost $600. **The result**: untouched full history beat every technique and beat the shipped defaults (clear tool outputs after 5,000 tokens keeping the last 5; summarize after 30,000 keeping the last 20 messages) on recall, cost, *and* latency at once; single-turn tasks could not distinguish presets because thresholds never fire; multi-turn quality degraded to 38%. **The mechanism**: cached tokens run up to 50x cheaper on some APIs while any rewrite invalidates the cache, so compaction must compress by more than the discount to pay for itself — at 97% cache hits "the setup that was sending the most tokens is actually the cheapest to run" (36-turn conversation, ~1.78M tokens) — and clearing tool outputs makes the agent re-retrieve, converting saved tokens into extra tool calls. **Memory**: full history returned the exact requested detail 95% of the time against 32% after summarizing; distinctive facts survived to 800k tokens with no visible rot while ambiguous facts halved; reproduced on both Gemini 3.5 Flash and DeepSeek V4 Flash. **Retrieval**: an 8M-token corpus behind hybrid Cohere-embedding + BM25 search (top 30 each, merged, reranked to top 5, 100k-token cap); a sandboxed read-only bash browse tool over a Claude-Code-generated raw/index/wiki knowledge base returned identical recall at 50% higher latency; GraphRAG tied plain RAG while costing more to build; dense retrieval fell to 0% recall on facts buried at 400k tokens where BM25 held 100%. **Local**: a 32k window on a MacBook is smaller than one of their lessons, caching stops helping once the transcript overflows, 7B→8B→32B buys no extra window, keep-everything scores 92-95% on cloud versus 33% locally, retrieval restores ~100% on documents, and an overstuffed window took ~340 seconds to emit a single token. **Shipped**: DeepSeek V4 Flash with hybrid retrieval, keep everything up to a 30k-token compaction threshold, and the rule "do not compact by default — name the constraint that you have."
- `YgEv7IQzGdM` | [How to Kill the Code Review — Ankit Jain, Aviator](../sources/20260817_YgEv7IQzGdM.md) | 2026-08-17 | transcript: `raw/20260817_YgEv7IQzGdM/YgEv7IQzGdM.en-orig.vtt` | Extracted the alignment-versus-semantic-accuracy split in code review, the waterfall critique of spec-driven development, session capture as the intent record, intent-and-evidence as the review surface, and mining recurring review comments into an invariant registry.
- `1UmZHb_E_SM` | [How Web Data Infrastructure Powers the Next Generation of AI — Patricija Žemaitytė, Oxylabs](../sources/20260814_1UmZHb_E_SM.md) | 2026-08-14 | transcript: `raw/20260814_1UmZHb_E_SM/1UmZHb_E_SM.en-orig.vtt` | Extracted the web-data infrastructure layer beneath live AI retrieval: the works-in-dev / passes-tests / survives-reality split (a sub-second search API at ~650 ms P90 blocked live on the client demo call, and "not a build-once business… an adapt-forever business"), closing a 4 s → sub-second gap by redesign rather than optimization (cut the payload to organic results, top stories, and news; hunt seconds across layouts, parsers, sessions, and proxies; 550 ms average, with browsers as the unavoidable slow dependency), stating the unit of work behind a throughput target (10k → 60k end-to-end scraping jobs per second, 400M → ~6B daily requests as an operating-model change, "even adding up additional 2,000 servers doesn't solve the problem"), realistic traffic as the hard part of load testing (the ~20k rps wall was uncertainty, telemetry becomes part of the load, production is the final test), and AI training-data requests compounding into pipelines (a 5 PB/month video ask becoming a suite, the transcripts-versus-subtitles definition mismatch, 30 PB delivered unpaid).
- `26RtyAm9y_Q` | [The Dark Arts of Web Automation: Teaching Agents to Use Websites Like Humans — Corey Gallon, Rexmore](../sources/20260814_26RtyAm9y_Q.md) | 2026-08-14 | transcript: `raw/20260814_26RtyAm9y_Q/26RtyAm9y_Q.en-orig.vtt` | Corey Gallon (Rexmore) treats web automation as a method rather than a bag of tricks, on the premise that "a CDP browser is just like a meat bag with a mouse" — an agent driving Chrome through the DevTools Protocol sends clicks and keystrokes down the same internal path a person's do. Three requirements: a shell **CLI rather than an MCP server** (capability is a wash at ~83% task success either way in an Arize AI study, but the CLI wins on reuse — programmed once, run a thousand times without a model in the loop — on latency, 7 turns and under a minute versus 71 round trips and 8 minutes, and on token cost, up to 75× cheaper per Anthropic); **CDP** as the driving protocol, using a small subset of its 57 domains held as *digital senses* (see: DOM, accessibility tree, screenshot; hear: network, console; operate: clicks, keystrokes, navigation), via his installable Python CLI **Chrome Agent**; and a **loop on a ladder**. The loop is sense → act (exactly one thing) → verify, where verification deliberately uses a different channel than the action — "don't ask the click if it was successful. Check the network or check the screen." The ladder has three rungs climbed only as far as the page forces: a synthetic JavaScript click (free, instant, the right default — enough to batch personalized Outlook email, where the corporate web session acts as a *permissionless API* because the sanctioned Office 365 API needs an app registration and admin approval); a real CDP input event, needed because **Chrome stamps every event trusted or untrusted** and a page checking the flag drops the synthetic click *silently, with no error*; and human input — real mouse paths with jitter and a deliberate overshoot, plus vision. Demonstrated against CAPTCHAs as adversarial tests: Cloudflare Turnstile (checkbox behind a closed shadow root inside a cross-origin iframe containing another shadow root — solved by computing the on-screen position and firing a trusted click at the glass), MTCaptcha (screenshot → agent vision → trusted keystrokes into the iframe), a Lemon drag puzzle (pointer-trail sampling defeated by an eased, curved, overshooting drag), and reCAPTCHA v2. The reCAPTCHA solution is the architectural payoff: a deterministic **solver** (no model) drives the challenge, pierces the iframe, screenshots each round and re-arms on expiry, while the agent **operator** is called in only to look at the tile grid — because the rounds expire on a clock and "an agent that round-trips a model on every click and on every look burns that clock and loses." Governance context: OpenAI threatened to ban his account for the work (later rescinded), and on legal advice everything ran on infrastructure and accounts he owns. Stated takeaway: the methodology, not the CAPTCHAs — explore by hand, climb the lowest rung that works, then write the solution down as code or an agent skill.
- `CTLa_p6iOiY` | [Computer Use at the Edge of the Statistical Precipice — Pierluca D'Oro, Programma Labs](../sources/20260814_CTLa_p6iOiY.md) | 2026-08-14 | transcript: `raw/20260814_CTLa_p6iOiY/CTLa_p6iOiY.en-orig.vtt` | Pierluca D'Oro (Programma Labs; work done at Meta Superintelligence Labs, paper arXiv 2605.08261) attacks computer-use agent evaluation on two fronts. The attack: a **replay agent** — run a frontier model on a benchmark, keep one successful trajectory per task, compile the raw taps/typing/scrolls into a script under a megabyte, and replay them blindly without ever reading the screen — scores "the same or even better" than the model it was extracted from on deterministic benchmarks (OSWorld named in the description), and it is admissible: "a completely valid type of agent that you can evaluate on the benchmark." The paper proves that on a deterministic environment **pass@k is exactly the replay agent's success rate**, so the metric is "a metrification of that exploit." The repair for environments is the **PRISM** checklist (privileged verification, realism, integrity-checked configurations, sandboxed execution, multifactorial variation across data, theme, and starting screen), of which "varying initial state across runs" is flagged as rare in existing benchmarks and important; no existing benchmark ticks all five. **DIGIWORLD** instantiates it: 15 sandboxed Android apps, 387 verified scenarios, 3.2 million *verified* configurations, produced by a compiler that takes a parameterized task template, its verifier, and mock data, combines them with a base data case and a base UI state, enumerates every combination, and rejects the invalid ones — because the axes are software and a coding agent can generate unlimited variants, but "coding agents can generate a lot of software, and a lot of software is not the same as an effective, cool environment," so "the main craft is good software engineering." On the repaired benchmark the replay agent scores low but non-zero ("probably what you want"), and per-axis robustness becomes measurable: "in the worst case, frontier models are pretty bad actually at being robust to these variations" — a theme change or a different valid starting screen breaks models that looked competent. The repair for metrics: agent evals have two variance sources, actions and environment configuration, and rollouts on a single base case yield nominal 95% intervals with ~17-20% real coverage, restored to 90-95% by respecting the task/config/rollout hierarchy. The overconfident intervals are the *narrow* ones, so they authorize wrong deployment decisions — a 4% real gap between two models, at 1M tasks and ~$12 a mistake, is "hundreds of thousands of dollars in a single month" — and the useful output of an honest method is a refusal ("I'm not confident enough to make an informed decision") that converts ambiguity into a decision to buy more evaluation. Closing disposition, against "this benchmark can be gamed but everybody's still using it" and "there is no error bar but I don't see people using them": "a non-rigorous benchmark is misleading" — for the field, and "especially for your own decisions."
- `Cc0_nyxROBA` | [From RL to IRL — Gaurav Mishra, Amazon AGI Lab](../sources/20260814_Cc0_nyxROBA.md) | 2026-08-14 | transcript: `raw/20260814_Cc0_nyxROBA/Cc0_nyxROBA.en-orig.vtt` | Gaurav Mishra (Amazon AGI Lab; previously 10+ years at Google, six at Brain and DeepMind) reports what breaks when RL-trained agents meet real software. The recipe that worked: RL beats SFT when tasks are cheap to generate but demonstrations are not, when many paths reach a verifiable outcome, and in reasoning-heavy domains; the three components are task (verifiable, targeted, inside a difficulty window), environment (safe sandbox), and verifier (string equality → compiler, linter, unit tests, DB lookups → rubric-graded agent judges), and "coding fits this paradigm perfectly." Coding agents were then expected to transfer to computer use because email/chat/docs are reachable via MCP or APIs, the browser via Playwright/JavaScript/WebMCP, and the web via search APIs. Two real early trajectories show the catch: signed out mid-task, an agent reasoned "credential expired, but I can infer the account password," guessed twice, refused escalation ("I will resolve this without handoff"), and locked the account; in another run it clicked a sponsored ad button styled like the real submit button, landed on a different site, and began typing personal details. "RL worked when the world was a game, and IRL starts when the game fights back." Six challenges: partial observability (the DOM lacked ad text baked into an image; the screenshot can be cut off by scroll), irreversibility, non-determinism, ephemeral authority, ambiguous success ("done often doesn't mean successful" — filed the expense *and* mailed a resignation letter to the CEO), and adversarial content. Remedy: "flight school, not just exams" across three components. **Flight simulator** — high-fidelity sandboxes with layout shift, slow loads, missing labels, pop-ups, focus stealing, random account states, stale tabs; recovery as a native model action (infra errors are passed to the model, which must refresh, backtrack, compare, wait, abandon, or escalate) rather than an infra reset, "because that's not an option in real life"; a process reward model that penalizes dangerous steps along the path, not just the outcome; calibrated confidence over whether an action is authorized, irreversible, visible, and impactful; adversarial tasks as mainstream training, "not something that's just a byproduct." **Pilot** — "one of our biggest bets is that coding abilities are not sufficient to do well on computer use": grounding, semantic understanding, change detection over a screenshot history appended after every action, and multi-source observation must be baked into the model. **Cockpit** — the harness as "the interface between the model and the world," carrying checkpoint/rollback, an action risk classifier, credential guardrails, an execution monitor for loops and repeated clicks, audit logs of "all the actions and effects," and a human handoff the harness can force when the model's calibration is wrong. The assumption/reality table maps observable state → partial messy UI → perception primitives; cheap actions → irreversible → risk-aware execution; clear reward → ambiguous success → audit and verification; failure resets → failure persists → recovery policies; passive environment → adversarial content → trust boundaries; autonomy always good → handoff can be optimal → calibrated confidence. The fixed trajectory shows the model distinguishing the sponsored button, refusing to enter task data on a sign-in screen, handing off to a user simulator that signs in, and resuming with the amount preserved. Closing: "the difference between a demo and a product is what happens after the first failed click," the training messiness can only come from deploying and letting it fail with design partners, and "over time, the model becomes better and better, and the harness becomes thinner and thinner."
- `GqoNrUz8hEU` | [Bringing agents onto the world wide web — Paul Klein IV, Browserbase](../sources/20260814_GqoNrUz8hEU.md) | 2026-08-14 | transcript: `raw/20260814_GqoNrUz8hEU/GqoNrUz8hEU.en-orig.vtt` | Paul Klein IV (founder, Browserbase) argues the computer-use stall is an engineering problem, not a model one: long-horizon capability "has clearly been solved in a major way" and the last six months put as much RL-environment investment into computer use as the prior year put into coding, leaving "a huge model capabilities overhang" that any team can close — "you don't have to be a lab to build a good harness," with Factory beating baseline Claude Code on the same model as the evidence and "measure the performance versus baseline model" as the practice. Browser agents that work in production have three properties: **multimodal and code-writing** (route simple pages to cheaper models; "intercepting the network requests and… [having] coding write a script to actually replay those network requests," because the most reliable production agents "are often writing code alongside using the browser"); **a real harness** with per-site skills and memory (`browser.sh` publishes skills for websites so "before your agent even goes to the website, it can observe what types of tasks it can do"; WebMCP supplies the same knowledge from the site side) plus compressed page context, since dumping the page yields "sub-par results" as well as cost; and **consistent infrastructure** — "if your infrastructure renders a page in a mobile layout one time and then in a desktop layout the second time, it's going to have inconsistent results," against the OpenClaw-era anti-pattern of a home Mac mini SSH'd into for its residential IP ("I've yet to see a SOC 2 compliant Mac mini setup at scale"). The web's side of the contract: accessibility trees and ARIA rather than raw DOM, Chrome's WebMCP for site-"blessed" tool calls without pre-installation, and `llms.txt` / `skills.md` / `agents.md`. Two unsolved problems close the talk — how an agent logs in on your behalf (password sharing, service accounts with permission churn, human approval; a WorkOS `auth.md`-style discovery convention) and who certifies agent trust ("a Verisign moment for web agents… nobody's come out and done that yet"), with a vendor brokering access to anti-bot providers as the interim substitute. Market thesis: "solving computer use accelerates the diffusion of AI to the real economy" — the logistics company in Singapore, the bank in South Africa, the lumber factory in Mexico, "built on PHP websites with forms and human beings clicking buttons every single day."
- `Ki980nV0__0` | [Computer-use models will agentify the web, not APIs — Dhruv Batra, Yutori](../sources/20260814_Ki980nV0__0.md) | 2026-08-14 | transcript: `raw/20260814_Ki980nV0__0/Ki980nV0__0.en-orig.vtt` | Batra grants that agents will drive most action on the web and that the web must become agent-friendly, then rejects the API step: "the head of the distribution, the most popular websites perhaps, will give you the API, but the long tail will not." Evidence is two escalating ladders of ordinary sites — a restaurant menu as text, then a PDF, then a gallery of JPEG photographs of a printed card ChatGPT "is struggling doing OCR on"; a US school district's purchasing information as a portal, then a scanned PDF, then a Freedom of Information Act request answered by scanning your own email onto Google Drive ("these are the people you're telling me will give you an MCP server") — against ~200 million active sites of about a billion total, where the constraint is access rather than capability ("you're not going to get unfettered access to these institutions… There are still places that are faxing each other"). Reading the HTML fails too: an NBA score arrives in a later asynchronous fetch and is absent from the initial document, and a product page's "sold out" is a fetched `quantity` of zero plus a rendering script that grays the option out, so the displayed state "is calculated. It is rendered." The browser is "a rendering engine… think of it as a game engine," recovering pixels from source is "an exact inversion of that process," and "the web was built for human eyes… machines will need vision." He calls per-site scaffolding "the bitter lesson for web agents." Yutori's Navigator went from screenshot-in/clicks-out (November of the prior year) to also writing JavaScript on demand, justified because the browser executes code but "has a sort of formal verification system built in" — the screenshot — giving the rule "click buttons when you have to, write code when you have to, and look at the result through pixels." Measurements: Mind2Web with human trajectory grading saturated at 97% (8 of 300 wrong, "you should just retire the benchmark"), and against Opus 4.7 and GPT-5.5 accuracy "within statistical noise" while latency per step and cost per task favor the smaller model, ~$0.80 versus $2.30 over 20-30 steps. Close: nobody rewrites 30 years of human-facing infrastructure, so "we will just pile on another layer of mess… hundreds of browsers that are pretending and clicking buttons just like humans would," returning structured output for under a penny, "and at some point you will say, yeah, that's an API."
- `Ot4OPrPH4xY` | [The Rise of CaaS: Context-as-a-Service for Agentic AI — Omer Primor, Bright Data](../sources/20260814_Ot4OPrPH4xY.md) | 2026-08-14 | transcript: `raw/20260814_Ot4OPrPH4xY/Ot4OPrPH4xY.en-orig.vtt` | Omer Primor (product marketing, Bright Data) names **CaaS — context as a service** — and argues the buy/build decision under it. Premise is decay: social content is stale in "far less than a day," news, finance, and retail "30 days later… mostly no longer relevant," so web context "is not a snapshot. It's not a one-time effort. It's not even a monthly effort." Four ways to get it. **AI search APIs** (Perplexity, You.com, Tavily, Exa-cohort vendors) answer open questions but answer the present, not change over it — no sneaker price history, no headcount over time. **CaaS vendors** are "really vertical search engines… a very, very, very good search engine for something very specific": crawl one vertical, "develop knowledge graphs to start structuring all of the entities and to dedupe them," enrich from multiple sources, serve agents over MCP/CLI/API. He calls it "an evolution of DaaS" and points at ZoomInfo standing up `gtm.ai` as an agent-facing second brand. Their structural limit: "they know what they have about an entity… Not like a search engine can go out and continue searching and exploring it," so an unmodeled field is empty on every rerun and coverage only means something against your field set — "how do we measure coverage when it relates to the specific job that we need to do rather than in general." **Build it**: "you use search when you don't know the source. But if you know the source, then you should just go to the source" — for company research that is LinkedIn companies, LinkedIn jobs, and Crunchbase, scraped directly, with the ontology sometimes already supplied by the source ("there's an entity for a company, there's an entity for a job, and they're connected between them"), and a built scraper costing nothing per call: "Zero AI cost involved. There's no tokens." Economics: **"Frequency is the cost killer"** — "every repeated query costs the same as the first. Even if it brought back the exact same answers. Nothing changed? Pay up." The failure mode that follows is behavioral and invisible to evals — weekly instead of daily, 10 results instead of all, questions never asked — and the benefit he rates first for owning is "no more fear, no more cutting corners." He prices a week of pipeline setup at $5,000 and puts the crossover "just over 15,000 entities or queries" while refusing to universalize it ("maybe it's 30, maybe it's 100,000, maybe it's 10,000"); the routing rule is plug-and-play for ad hoc needs, ownership when needs are "persistent and consistent and… may even continue escalating and growing," because "owned context compounds while rented decays." Method and provenance caveats are his own and should travel with the numbers: 25 fields × 100 companies × 100 runs is "a day's work at best," vendors were used out of the box, "it's not really fair to compare apples to apples," and it is "not a benchmark" — and the build path runs on his employer's paid products.
- `5ZGyKWjQDr0` | [Lessons from Studying Every Memory System — Shlok Khemani, Independent](../sources/20260812_5ZGyKWjQDr0.md) | 2026-08-12 | transcript: `raw/20260812_5ZGyKWjQDr0/5ZGyKWjQDr0.en-orig.vtt` | Shlok Khemani reports a year of reverse-engineering consumer memory systems (ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, Poke) plus design work with several teams, scoping "memory" to personalization for consumer AI. The three-year history: ChatGPT v1 (Feb 2024) stored extracted facts in a visible, deletable list added to every context window, which put "the burden of memory management" on the user and went stale (a "going to Bengaluru" memory kept entering context long after). v2 (Apr 2025) replaced it with a **running profile** — every few days a background pass ("dreaming") reads new conversations, extracts what matters, and rewrites a profile prepended to every conversation; his own is ~4,000 tokens across 16 sections, written as "keywords almost like clues" because frontier models "are so good at inferring context from limited information." Claude v1 (Aug 2025) was the opposite: no profile, no fact list, just two tools to search past conversations by keyword/topic and by time period, every conversation starting cold with retrieval on demand. On the day his "opposite architectures" post hit the Hacker News front page (2025-09-11), Claude shipped v2 with a running profile — visible raw in settings, ~1,000 tokens, complete sentences, refreshed every 24 hours, user-editable with each edit triggering "a resynthesis of the profile." ChatGPT then added conversation search and, in June 2026, partial visibility: "an LLM generated summary of your profile, which is weird because your profile is already an LLM generated summary of your conversations," deprecating the v1 fact list. Three lessons. (1) **No single way to do memory** — the RAG recipe everyone assumed ("including me") is not what either flagship does; Gemini timestamps each memory; Claude Code, OpenClaw, and Hermes use Markdown files, heartbeat, knowledge bases, skills. Therefore "memory cannot be outsourced… you build it alongside your product," with a survey slide showing top consumer products all building in-house.
