(2) **Memory is a function of compute** — maintenance cost (update frequency × compute per update) versus serving cost (the profile enters every context window), with ChatGPT (4,000 tokens / every few days) and Claude (1,000 tokens / 24 hours) at opposite corners of one budget; unconstrained he would update after every conversation and store 400,000 tokens, but "we live in a GPU constrained world." (3) **Continual learning is already here**, outside the weights: profile → conversations → synthesis → profile. Moving it into weights is blocked economically — enterprise training amortizes "across different employees, different customers, but that's not the case at an individual level" — leaving who-pays open. The closing rant is a ceiling argument: the best architecture plus infinite compute is still "capped by how much context it can gather about you." His profile says he went to Turkey in 2025 (he went to Thailand; the entry came from conversations comparing them), the deciding conversation happened in person, and the settling evidence — flight and hotel bookings — sits in an inbox ChatGPT is connected to but "doesn't reason over." What bothers him is that nothing notices the contradiction and nothing is "curious about trying to fill in gaps": "it's not a technology problem. It's a product problem." Meanwhile each chatbot, assistant, vertical app, agent, and device builds a private memory, shares none, and must be updated by hand — "none of this feels like 2026… when will personal AI feel like personal AI?" Closing note: memory for AI is "just a three-year-old field."
- `CvRngaQZQ3Y` | [Improving Agents is a Data Mining Problem — Vivek Trivedy, LangChain](../sources/20260812_CvRngaQZQ3Y.md) | 2026-08-12 | transcript: `raw/20260812_CvRngaQZQ3Y/CvRngaQZQ3Y.en-orig.vtt` | Vivek Trivedy (applied research, LangChain) proposes a four-step improvement loop — ship the agent, collect traces, mine the corpus, run data-driven experiments — on the claim that "there's a very tight coupling between what observability is and what continual learning is… if you're a continual learning company, you need traces." Traces matter more for agents than for code because "agents have prompts, they have tools, they have skills, they have hooks, they have middlewares, some agents call other agents and I orchestrate them in swarms," and because "over the last four years since the ChatGPT moment, we've started trading determinism for autonomy." The questions he says only traces can answer come in three shapes: which users are upset and which are happy; behavioral degradation such as "Does the agent get really dumb after the first compaction? After the second compaction?"; and counterfactuals like GPT-5.5 versus GLM 5.2 on the same task. The read path is the constraint — cost is "the input token cost times the number of traces times like how big each trace is on average," and a single trace can already overflow a window ("I can't even read that trace with another agent because that context like doesn't fit in memory") — so the practice is "we send agents to read traces from other agents" and treating context "as like an external object and then I can sort of query into it," with the forward claim that "the data that we see today is going to be the smallest that humans have ever seen in their entire lives." The model-selection method is prove-then-descend: "what is the minimum level of intelligence that I need to do any given task… we start with Opus, we start with 5.5 because we just want to know if the task is even possible," establish that waterline, then "look back at those traces" for the harness guidance a cheap model needs ("Opus reasons about things in this way… I need to give it a little bit more guidance"). His reported instance is a Harvey legal benchmark where an open model matched Opus at trace-judging "at like an order or like two orders of magnitude cheaper" — one vendor-reported engagement, no published methodology, a 10× band inside the claim. Past the harness ceiling ("if I keep tweaking this prompt, I'm not going to get too much more out of it") he argues for fine-tuning a narrow vertical, justified because customers "don't really care about the entire variance of tasks," with the aim to "reach and then also go beyond frontier performance" and the accounting consequence that for "very high inference workloads" you "move from token costs to hardware costs" — "I run a cluster and I get like unlimited inference," spun down when idle. LangChain's mining product reads all traces, finds issues, searches them agentically, and prepares datasets with three outputs: distillation/SFT from good GLM 5.2 traces into 9B and 13B models; generated evals and environments, on the observation that "you can basically define agent behavior by showing the evals that you ran on it… it literally like hill climbs those evals"; and human-readable content, because "humans are still in the loop" and "in like high-trust domains like legal and medical… some human needs to review this, but they can't read it all." The unifying frame is borrowed from scikit-learn's `fit()`: **model-harness-task fit**, "take my data, take a harness, take a model, and I'm going to try to fit it all together," leaving two jobs — find good fit functions and find good data — with the warning that "agents are pretty good at making that number go up. They might cheat a little bit." Two operational rules close it. Sequence by feedback speed: "harness engineering gives you feedback in maybe 2 minutes" against hours or days for training, so the sandwich is harness → fine-tune → harness again, and "a lot of teams are happy with harness engineering" as an end state. And densify: when "the output is just a number," you learn the run failed but not where — "densifying feedback is a really good way to improve agents, and like traces are the substrate that hold that feedback." The three axes he says an agent updates along are training data, harness ("they look a certain way because like models are trained in them"), and memory, where he rejects the naive shape — "we are not append-only logs of information… we cannot just append everything to a really big file and then search over it" — and points at "scaling sleep time compute and dreaming." Takeaway as given: "just turn on tracing and point an agent at it." Provenance: the speaker leads applied research at LangChain, the talk ends on a product pitch plus an offer to fine-tune open models as a service, and captions garble the product name and several RL method names, which the source note leaves unresolved.
- `I3bpdgFJCUY` | [LLM Knowledge Bases: a practical guide — Ben Holmes, Warp](../sources/20260812_I3bpdgFJCUY.md) | 2026-08-12 | transcript: `raw/20260812_I3bpdgFJCUY/I3bpdgFJCUY.en-orig.vtt` | Ben Holmes (developer relations lead at Warp) demonstrates a four-stage personal knowledge-base pipeline — capture, enrich, generate, visualize — where the human does only the first stage. He locates the bottleneck in capture rather than organization: speech runs "like 200 words per minute, I believe, is the average. Unless you're an absolute Olympic typist, it will be faster than any other method that you have," so he dictates everything, on desktop or phone, and keeps it deliberately rough — "don't worry if you're being a little bit scrappy, a little bit rambly... The goal should just be get down as many thoughts in the moment as possible" — because the generated layers need supply: "you need a lot of raw data. You need a lot of raw materials." Local, subscription-free dictation options named: **Handy** (open source, local model, on device) and **VoiceInk** (captioned "Voice Inc.", ~$20 lifetime, hotkey plus mobile app). Structure then arrives as agent passes over the same Markdown. An `enrich note` **skill** does four things: stamps an enrichment timestamp "so if we ask the agent to do another pass it remembers that some other agent did it in the past" (which makes the batch run incremental — it finds "any notes that weren't enriched in the past"); assigns tags from a fixed **reference folder** rather than inventing them, with the instruction "to be reluctant to add new tags because Claude loves to get creative" plus an escape hatch for genuine new patterns; researches the source URL with web tools ("exa is a great little tool that you can add into your harness"); and writes **backlinks** found by plain key-term search into the bottom of each note — "use some file calls, find related notes using key term search, and put them into the bottom" — no embeddings, and "that web gets tighter and tighter the more things that you get down." The enriched layer pays off before any wiki: notes on one book taken weeks apart link together, so "I'm not hunting around where did I save in my Apple Notes the last time I wrote about this," and "it kind of invents a Wikipedia rabbit hole of your own thoughts." A second pass generates an **entity wiki** — "a set of people, places, and things basically": sources, topics, concepts, people, organizations — scoped to a stated focus area, crediting a gist by Andrej Karpathy (captioned "Andre Carpathy"): "this is where the LLM knowledge base idea kind of came together... search his name and then wiki." The prompt was that plain: "these are the things that I care about. They're in this raw folder. Here's the rest of his gist. Go ahead and generate a wiki for me." His justification: "all I have time to do is generate the raw ingredients not connecting it all together myself." Two demos — an AI-news wiki surfacing a Ralph-loop entry and a jazz musician on AI in music, and a Bible-in-a-Year wiki whose payoff is a per-character entry where "all the names sound very similar and can be hard to pronounce" — plus the work version, a people section of everyone met with, backlinked to meetings and down to raw notes. The loop then leaves the laptop, for a stated reason: "it can take time for an agent to go generate these things," so run it on a cadence "maybe while we sleep." He draws the local/cloud line on a competitor: the Codex app's automations "spin up tasks to run on your machine every day, but it means your laptop has to be cracked open when it runs because it's a local automation." His version stands up a cloud sandbox daily (Docker-backed if needed), syncs the Markdown down with the **Obsidian headless CLI**, runs the skill over unenriched notes, and syncs back — preferred over the alternative he names ("you could also just do like a git clone... put all of your notes in a GitHub folder") because it "avoids having to like push and pull your notes. No one has time for that." The output is a review artifact: "I wake up to a perfectly fresh wiki that I can review. It's like the daily paper, but it's your own," with runs still viewable in the browser. Finally, past Markdown: he asked an agent to build a clickable HTML/Tailwind force graph over every note — "this is not a tool that you have to install. By the way, I told an agent, build this for me" — restyled it into a star constellation on request and added a GitHub-style habit chart, using it diagnostically: clusters for books, startup founding, AI and engineering, faith and scripture, and "it's useful just to get an idea of what you're actually interested in and where you have gaps in your thinking." Provenance: Warp DevRel; the harness (Warp), scheduler (oz.dev / warp.dev/os), and notes app (open-source Hubble / hub.md) are all first-party, and nothing in the talk is measured — no error rates, costs, latencies, comparisons, or corpus size.
- `I6aiEf3aEFQ` | [Intelligence + Continual Learning = Expertise — Yu Su, NeoCognition](../sources/20260812_I6aiEf3aEFQ.md) | 2026-08-12 | transcript: `raw/20260812_I6aiEf3aEFQ/I6aiEf3aEFQ.en-orig.vtt` | Yu Su (professor at Ohio State; COO at NeoCognition, "we focus on agents and continual learning") gives an explicitly conceptual talk separating two things he says the field collapses. **Intelligence** is "the capacity to reason through unfamiliar problems from available context" — what frontier models are increasingly good at, where "every episode is more or less independent from each other." **Expertise** is "accumulated and situated competence… the ability to act reliably, efficiently, and with judgment to achieve [reproducibly superior] performance in a particular domain" (captions garble that phrase). His account of the coding-agent gap is a modern Moravec's paradox — "hard things are easy, easy things are hard": code is "already a language-native world. Everything is already represented symbolically and recorded in a very structured way. And you get your rewards, you get your tests all in place in symbolic ways," while everyday digital work "require[s] quite different set of cognitive competencies." Leaving code goes badly ("Well, not so well"), and he keeps Andrew Ng's "decade of agents" line on the grounds that computer use and continual learning are "still largely the same right now." The structural reason: society "is really not just one unified world. It's millions of these micro worlds… even if you're using the same software, every company configure it differently," each with "its unique local physics, like different structures, constraints, affordances, and dynamics that you have to learn" — "too heterogeneous and dynamic for any monolithic model to try to compress it into one static representation," so "agents must continually learn on the job to acquire… specialized expertise for each specific microworld." What expertise contains, from cognitive science: experts "don't just know more facts. They actually see the world differently" — pattern recognition (a huge bug report, and they "immediately locate the most plausible places where things could go wrong"), deep structure (scheduling a meeting is "a constraint optimization problem over everyone's authority, the priorities, the urgency"), conditionality ("every rule has like the preconditions where it applies, but then we also know when we can bend the rules"), and judgment/taste including "when to stop, when it's good enough" — amounting to "a world model of their environments." Operationally: intelligence solves "through the context," but "expertise actually will bring you the right context"; and since "every problem solving is a search problem," intelligence "tends to brute force it — spin up like 100 different parallel ways," while "expertise will actually try to compress the search space," which is his answer to the talk's opening puzzle about token inefficiency. Continual learning is "the important bridge from intelligence to expertise," and because the term "is such a confusing term" he defines it: **"adaptive compression of experience into reusable structures for future behavior,"** with four open axes — experience (episodes / semantic facts / procedures / feedback), compression (vectors, symbolic index, distillation into parameters, RL) which "needs to be adaptive" since "what you have compressed so far should largely influence how you compress further," structure (adapters, vectors, graphs, skills, or world models), and use (recall, prediction of future states, planning, actuation, or a value function). The cross-product "makes this field so confusing." "Maybe the most important figure in this talk" plots raw intelligence against expertise and finds them "largely orthogonal": with no continual learning you get "the world's smartest novice… it doesn't accumulate expertise, so it ends up as just like brute forcing its way at every problem," and the CL algorithm "set[s] the slope of your learning curve" (caveat: "assuming like a given time horizon and the experience horizon"). The provocation is unbounded expertise from bounded intelligence — "once the raw intelligence has [crossed] a certain threshold, we don't need a stronger intelligence anymore" — hence "do we need to continually train these larger and larger models? Or… maybe they're already good enough. What we're missing is just like better continual learning algorithms." Open questions he leaves: how to "define and measure expertise" (probably environment-specific); reliability versus plasticity, "inherently conflicting" since "stable systems… resist the change. But the plastic systems likes change," with humans as "a living existence proof"; and how to "synergize" parametric and non-parametric learning, which he believes are both needed. Closing: specialization may "lead to like better generalization," and since "we have exhausted the public data for training LLMs… the next internet-scale data opportunity is actually in all of these different private worlds," where specialized agents "learn in situ and channel back the learning to the general model." Call to action: "let's start scaling expertise… intelligence is already becoming abundance… But expertise is still scarce," every company building "their own learning loop" (citing Satya Nadella on institutional memory) and "their own [moats]" while staying "in charge of their means of production," so that lowered friction moves "many of the new type of work across the threshold of worth doing." Provenance: a position talk with no measurement, benchmark, or system result; the only figure quoted (Anthropic's revenue growing "400 times to 40 billion," "maybe 60 billion annualized") is secondhand and illustrative; the speaker is COO of a company whose product direction is the talk's conclusion. Caption artifacts recorded on the source note include "Isu" for Yu Su, "The Neo Cognition" for NeoCognition, "word models" for world models, "own modes" for moats, and an unresolved "escape intelligence" that most likely intends escape velocity.
- `R3-anFK1YM8` | [Memory Harnesses for Long-Running Research Agents — Stefania Druga, Sakana.ai](../sources/20260812_R3-anFK1YM8.md) | 2026-08-12 | transcript: `raw/20260812_R3-anFK1YM8/R3-anFK1YM8.en-orig.vtt` | Stefania Druga (research scientist, Sakana AI, Tokyo) reports a single-author ablation of *memory harnesses* run entirely on local models on her own machine. The mental model she asks for: memory is a **write–manage–read loop** — "it's not just a database store. It's actually this control loop around the model" — built from a **core** "always shown to the agent," a **recall** block "where I'm testing different modes," and an **archival** block "keeping track of information across different sessions." She starts from research agents "because they have zero durable memory, and I wanted all the memory to come from the harness," and "the model is fixed across all the different tasks. So the only things that I'm changing is like these different variables in the recall block." The recall ladder: no recall at all; "a vector rag… just to see whatever the harness would pull in terms of similarity"; "a decisions ledger where I actually keep track of what decisions are being made for every turn, and then I can prioritize them"; and "what I call an oracle, but basically this is the ground truth… telling the harness for every loop what the correct memory that needs to be retrieved is." Two tasks bracket the boundary. **Task one, a null result**: a literature review whose corpus held a Nature claim of "742,000 promising materials" later retracted, where "the retraction, it's a much smaller haystack needle in that corpus than the headlines and the citations" — yet "because for these tasks, all the papers and all the information fit into the context, the memory actually didn't add more capability. It was the same performance with memory and without memory, and it only added more cost. So when your task fits in context, the harness doesn't add much." **Task two, from xbench** (captioned "X-Bench"), inverts it: "the right answer is in like step 124, but the moment when I ask the question, I'm asking it like at step 500. So it's completely outside of the context window, and the model needs to use the memory harness to retrieve the specific answer from the right step." Over "68 questions" with "multiple cells and lots of different seeds," "the rank only ledger performed the best. And it performed better than like just gating the harness by saying do you need to use memory or do you not need to use memory." The oracle notably does not reach the ceiling: it "provides the right information, the right memory to the model, but it doesn't force it to use it. So the model can get the right memory but still retrieve the wrong information or choose to ignore it or be confused." Ablations feeding arbitrary examples, the wrong step, and the most recent step left "the ranked policy for recall" on top, and the ordering held on a second local model (DeepSeek V4 Flash alongside one captioned "Qwen 27B") and a second benchmark (Spider V2). The cost result: "it's not just that it gives you better recall, it actually costs less," compressed into the heuristic "bad memory is expensive because it spends more token and it can send the agent the wrong way. But having like a good structural policy for recall can save you a lot of tokens and budget." Her ask is to treat "the recall policy as a first-class metric" and answer four design questions explicitly: what memories to store, how to rank them, how to design the recall function, and "what survives when you run this over and over and over and multiple sessions, multiple runs?" Framing: METR projections show task horizons lengthening while "we're getting fewer and fewer model releases," converging on "many more long-term horizon tasks and fewer model releases," which makes context rot — "the model starts contradicting itself, or it has to redo the work because it forgot it did that task in the first place, or it starts to drift from your questions" — "a priority." A second-hand Coinbase datapoint (CEO tweet, no figures) reports reduced AI spend with increased usage via more local models plus "better routing, better caching, keeping the context clean" and per-task visibility. Rig: an M3 Ultra, 96 GB, 28 CPU cores, driven from her phone in Tokyo, with fans stacked around it after days of non-stop evals. Local bought "the data I was using, the entire traces of compute and evaluations… an example of sovereignty" and cost serial execution — "they don't support batch querying for the deep seek 4 flash" — which is why evals were still running during her flight. Provenance and limits: single-author unpublished work in a 13-minute talk, closing on Sakana's sovereign-AI positioning and a hiring call; **no effect sizes are reported anywhere** — every result is an ordering, with no variance or significance test; all measurements are on local quantized models; and the ranking function inside the winning ledger is never described.
- `WiqDvX6isc4` | [Scaling Compute on Context — Jack Morris, Engram](../sources/20260812_WiqDvX6isc4.md) | 2026-08-12 | transcript: `raw/20260812_WiqDvX6isc4/WiqDvX6isc4.en-orig.vtt` | Jack Morris, researcher at the startup Engram (launched the week before), frames private-corpus adaptation and maps where each current approach stops. The frame is Terence Tao on breadth against depth: AI "knows like every single public mathematical topic" and can bridge literature gaps "in a way that no human even can know because it's read so much," but lacks the depth of "a graduate student who spent 5 years practicing in one area." Scaling compute on context is "the pursuit of depth in AI." The structural argument: the three axes behind "really the entirety of the deep learning revolution" — more data, longer training, bigger models — all run on public data, and so does the purchased post-training layer from **Scale AI, Surge AI, and Mercor**, "still by definition creating publicly available data because it's something that the model could tell to a user." So models keep improving at "coding in the way that is public on GitHub" while "not getting more knowledge of you or your life or your work." Against your own corpus the data axis is gone ("we can't create new data") and training from scratch is out, which "leaves us with essentially one axis of scaling, which is compute." Stated formally: one pre-trained model θ plus one unstructured D ("all the emails you've ever written… all the transcripts from every meeting your company's ever had"), produce a θ* that "knows" D — with "know" flagged as "a very load-bearing term." Four families, each with its stopping point. **Naive next-token training**: 10K financial reports, "a loss of like 0.0001," a model that "knows the data perfectly well," and then "when you generate from it, it basically collapses" — bounded *and* non-generalizing, since it "can't answer any question unless the question is perfectly encoded in the data with its answer." **KV compaction** ("similar to the way that… Claude code or Codex or open code… does compaction"): "it only applies to things that are in context" and "misses… the magic that you can get from taking gradients." **On-policy distillation** ("you make the model think that the text is in context"): works, but "you can't really distill the raw documents," which is the gap self-study in the **cartridges paper** fills by generating Q/A pairs conditioned on D. **Continued pre-training on synthetic data**: promising, but "you sort of overwrite some of the pre-training," it is "difficult to scale," and "you then have to post-train the model after doing this" while "a lot of people don't actually start with good pre-trained base models." Unsupervised RL environments with a GRPO-style loss are the same shape. The shared ceiling is the talk's contribution: "whatever you do, you have to define the data set, and then you train on the data set, and eventually things saturate… unless your model is under parameterized, eventually it will learn all the data" — "kind of like a data wall in the synthetic sense," after which "you have to do it again" and "what's this second stage of training look like?" is the open question. The missing property is AlphaGo's: it "makes its own training questions harder by getting better through training." Engram's own early curves "plateau because there's this almost like natural upper bound to how much you can learn in one go from D"; the claimed fix ("more sophisticated things… that make the training gradually harder") is asserted without description. One aside cuts against the whole framing: "the amount of data isn't really fixed," because studying a textbook you can "find other textbooks," search the internet, and "be proactive and talk to speakers of the language or people who know the thing." Provenance: a framing talk ("not… a super detail-oriented talk") by a researcher at a company selling this problem's solution, closing on job postings; **no results are reported for any method, including his own**, and the only Engram datapoint is a negative one. Caption artifacts resolved: "N gram"/"Ngram" is Engram, "Meter" is METR, "archive" is arXiv, "GBT" is GPT, "open code" is OpenCode. Left flagged: "10K financial reports" (ten thousand reports or SEC 10-K filings), the loss value (captions say 0.0001, the description says 0.00001), and a first-name-only attribution of on-policy distillation to another speaker at the same event.
- `XEd_SRVHBgU` | [Adaption Labs: Gradient-Free Continual Learning — Sara Hooker, Adaption](../sources/20260812_XEd_SRVHBgU.md) | 2026-08-12 | transcript: `raw/20260812_XEd_SRVHBgU/XEd_SRVHBgU.en-orig.vtt` | Sara Hooker, founder of Adaption and formerly head of Cohere Labs, argues that the "unreasonably narrow path" AI development has taken — a handful of labs, one model served to everyone, scale as the lever — is an artifact of who could afford to ask questions rather than of what works, and that it is now opening. The engineering content is her company's AutoScientist: an automated search over model-training configurations that co-optimizes data with the model ("we did not get the returns… until you control for data quality") and is reported to beat internal research staff on 60-something percent of runs. That figure is the most instructive thing in the talk for a careful reader, because she also states the exit condition — the search is given a budget and stops once it beats the baseline — which makes the one-sided clustering just above a round number a property of the stopping rule rather than a capability ceiling, generalizing to best-of-N, agent retry loops, and any swept benchmark. Two stated mechanisms for the edge: the search carries no favorite architecture family (dense vs. MoE, size), and it will move many hyperparameters at once where a person moves one. The macro argument is the "Slow Death of Scaling": pre-training size has saturated as the most lucrative axis while post-training keeps "moving further back," so the interesting compute is the distributable kind (inference, post-training) rather than the colocated kind pre-training requires — which she reads as a claim about market entry, since fewer than 5,000 people worldwide have trained a frontier model and that know-how is apprenticeship, passed by working beside someone, not literature. Design decisions taken from day one: non-verifiable tasks, because they are "the bulk of… everyday tasks," and 242 languages. Two boundaries are drawn in Q&A and both are load-bearing: pointing the same search at agent harnesses works only if the model moves with it, because search against frozen weights converges; and customization control is a separate question from open weights — "whether that's local or private or within that company, it's about… how do they own their own intelligence?" — three axes (weight distribution, adaptation control, deployment locality) that policy and procurement language routinely collapse into one. Provenance is heavy: this is a founder talk with a free-GPU beta pitch, the win rates and the "outperforms research staff" claim are unpublished, the one piece of external evidence offered for the size-ceiling claim is a single Open LLM leaderboard flip at a single parameter cut, and the coverage claims are stated intent. The talk title's "gradient-free" never appears in the transcript; it is event framing, not a described method.
- `ZTA0GwpAUak` | [Bringing Continual Learning into Enterprises — Samuel Denton, Applied Compute](../sources/20260812_ZTA0GwpAUak.md) | 2026-08-12 | transcript: `raw/20260812_ZTA0GwpAUak/ZTA0GwpAUak.en-orig.vtt` | Samuel Denton (Applied Compute) gives the wiki's most operational account of enterprise continual learning, and its organizing artifact is a 2x2. Two independent axes govern any such setup: how on-policy the *trace* is (a one-time offline production dump → a daily batch → the "unified engine" where serving and training are one loop, which he calls "the holy grail") and where the *hint* comes from (a static offline prior about desired behavior vs. a hint constructed dynamically against the model's own rollout). Applied Compute works the two diagonal corners — offline/offline and online/online — and the grid separates what is an infrastructure prerequisite from what is a supervision-design choice. The supervision half rests on one idea: the teacher is made smarter than the student not by being bigger but by holding information the student lacked, which lets self-distillation run with no golden answer and no rubric. Two customer results carry the talk. First, a customer wanted a coding agent to call a specific tool more often; the teacher's conditioning trace never contained the call — "we're never actually conditioning on the tokens of the tool call itself" — so the update landed on the deliberation and the action followed, moving a long-horizon SWE-bench-style task-completion rate from roughly 22% to 60% with the test pass rate flat. Second, a customer harness required an unusual hyperlink format, and both obvious fixes failed: a format reward (correct and not hackable) and SFT on correct traces each degraded general coding ability, while a hint written against the agent's own prior rollout took correctness from ~15% to ~80% — and the same instruction applied uniformly across every step improved things only "a little bit," which locates the win in placement rather than content. Three nested localization tricks follow from that: an LLM judge picks where in the rollout the hint goes, distillation runs on that step and a few after (the KL signal decays with distance), and relevance-masked self-distillation uses a judge to select which teacher tokens are learned from at all, since "the teacher model has preferences of certain connector words that are not really relevant." The measurement discipline is stated as three metrics held jointly — behavior rate, base-task rate measured "irrespective of" the behavior, and their intersection. Read against the same company's December 2025 talk on pipeline RL, which graded final answers, this is the same vendor eight months later designing around not having an answer to grade. Provenance: a vendor talk closing on a hiring slide; every number comes from unpublished internal customer runs with no seeds, variance, or sample counts; the quadrant-3 claim (a single on-policy step beats fully offline) is delivered against a chart with no numbers spoken; the two failed baselines are reported without magnitudes; there is no cost accounting for the online loop; and the relevance-masking mechanism is deferred to an uncited blog post.
- `iqloyWCGYQQ` | [Beyond Static Intelligence: Evaluating Continual Learning — Parth Asawa, UC Berkeley](../sources/20260812_iqloyWCGYQQ.md) | 2026-08-12 | transcript: `raw/20260812_iqloyWCGYQQ/iqloyWCGYQQ.en-orig.vtt` | Parth Asawa (UC Berkeley, with Snorkel AI and UW–Madison) presents Continual Learning Bench 1.0 and, more durably, the metric it is built around. The problem statement: every standard benchmark scores instances independently, which tells the model "imagine that every time you do something, you completely forget your memory," so no existing number says whether a system improves with experience. The instrument is **gain** — stateful reward minus stateless reward, obtained by running each system through the whole benchmark twice, once carrying state across instances and once reset between every one. That control removes the confound that "total reward alone might confound continual learning ability with base model strength," and it is deliberately agnostic about what "state" means: "it might mean that it's updating its notes. It might mean that it's just growing its context length," which is what lets notepads, summarizers, retrievers, and a raw growing window be compared on one plot. Three design criteria a task must meet before a learning score means anything: **headroom** in the strict sense that the gap must require *online* adaptation rather than being closable by more offline training; **shared latent structure** across instances so there is something to carry; and a **learning signal** in the environment (a scalar reward, an error message, textual feedback). The cheapest shortcut is ruled out explicitly — chaining existing benchmarks fails because their instances "are designed to be independent," so a chained run produces gain that is noise around zero. The suite spans six domains (blind spectrum monitoring, codebase adaptation, cohort studies/epidemiology, exploitable poker, database exploration, sales prediction), is expert-validated, and deliberately injects **concept drift**: a mid-run database migration that drops and renames columns and changes formats, so the eval tests what a system stops believing rather than only what it accumulates. The headline result cuts against the field's direction of travel: "vanilla in context learning where you just put the experience in the context and you don't do any of the fancy context management" topped the leaderboard on reward and sat on both Pareto frontiers (reward-vs-cost and gain-vs-cost), beating more expensive memory and context-management systems. Winning on *gain*, not just reward, is what rules out the base-model explanation. Asawa raises the objection himself and calls it fair: these were "medium horizon tasks" that "didn't push the frontiers of the in-context learning systems enough." The failure analysis sorts onto the classical axis with two observable trace signatures — a **stability** failure where a sales forecast oscillates back to its original overprediction because the system responds only to the latest feedback, and a **plasticity** failure where a notepad agent writes "These seem to be cohort definitions from a different study schema that doesn't apply here" about a schema that did apply. The closing argument is a research direction stated as one: today's methods are all bolted onto frozen checkpoints from models "never designed to be continual learners in the first place," and the alternative shape is "one phase of training which is training for continual learning and then everything after that is deployment" — proposed with no method and no evidence. Working definition offered: continual learning is "sample efficient online learning that is stable over long horizons," pursued through in-context, external-memory, and parametric mechanisms. Provenance: an academic talk funded in part by a Snorkel AI open-benchmarks grant and the [Laude] Institute slingshots program, presenting the speaker's own benchmark. **No numbers appear anywhere in the talk** — not the leaderboard, not the gain values, not the cost axis — and the competing systems are never named, so the headline result cannot be checked or attributed. The doubled evaluation cost of the two-run protocol goes unmentioned, parametric methods are only partially represented, and each failure mode is illustrated by a single anecdote. Caption artifacts resolved: "Partasawa" → Parth Asawa, "Amy data sets"/"AM problems" → AIME, "per room" → headroom, "Parto"/"PTO Frontiers" → Pareto frontiers, "RHF" → RLHF, "multi-teer" → multi-teacher, "Udub" → UW, "know which is" → "now which is". Left unresolved: "LOD institute" (rendered as [Laude] Institute, unconfirmed) and "blind spectrum monitoring" as a domain name.
- `jHMiYtjoJfA` | [Designing Agents (The Floor Is the Frontier) — Ben Hylak, Raindrop](../sources/20260812_jHMiYtjoJfA.md) | 2026-08-12 | transcript: `raw/20260812_jHMiYtjoJfA/jHMiYtjoJfA.en-orig.vtt` | Ben Hylak (Raindrop) argues that agent quality should be scored on the floor — "the worst thing your agent can do" — rather than the ceiling, and that the two are inseparable because the same creative-under-obstruction behavior produces both. Extracted the ceiling/floor split and its floor examples (recommend a competitor, delete data, send an AI slop email "because it technically had access"); the user-responsibility spectrum from tab complete to AI doctor, which is also why lab eval vocabulary misfires downstream; the two triage numbers (onset and share of users); the three structural reasons clusters cannot serve as an issue tracker; code mode transplanted from MCP tool calling to sandboxed trace classifiers at production scale; the deterministic-detection / agentic-investigation split; the ~80% eval-suite invalidation on a harness switch and the two-week model-upgrade-delay test; evals as repo-resident tests rather than hosted prompt playgrounds; and user count as the gate on which quality methods exist at all. Vendor talk with no figures from Raindrop's book of business; the 80% is an explicit estimate, the anomaly-detection claim carries no evidence, and the market claims come from a self-selected customer base.
- `zL1kLftVTlo` | [Scaling up Continual Learning — Ronak Malde, Trajectory](../sources/20260812_zL1kLftVTlo.md) | 2026-08-12 | transcript: `raw/20260812_zL1kLftVTlo/zL1kLftVTlo.en-orig.vtt` | Ronak Malde (Trajectory) gives the second independent account of hint-based on-policy self-distillation at the same event, and his contribution is a comparison rubric, a mechanism argument, and two failure modes with mitigations. The rubric scores any post-training algorithm on four properties — is the task distribution online, is sampling on-policy, is parallelism one, is the reward per token — and walks SFT, DPO/RLHF, and GRPO through it, framing GRPO as a deliberate trade ("we basically took a Fouian [Faustian] bargain and wanted to max on on policy rollouts… our parallelism is now exploded, meaning that we need really robust environment infrastructure"). Two rows carry the weight: parallelism is the only property that translates directly into a budget line, since group rollouts are what force environments that are "one-to-one copies of the real world," and signal density gets the sharpest argument — a sequence-level scalar is "almost like you're drinking through a straw," as if "you were trying to write an essay and your teacher just gave you a score of 87 out of 100." The mechanism claim is that a distillation loss matches the teacher's full next-token distribution rather than reweighting the token sampled, so "it's actually not just the top token that you sampled that you're actually making better, but instead the entire vocabulary… we're not just taking now a distribution like RL and slightly sharpening it, but we're instead actually shifting entire distributions," with the reported consequence that GRPO "saturates around sonnet level" on LiveCodeBench while distillation reaches "brand new territory," and the more checkable corollary that tokens-to-solve *collapses* rather than inflating. The failure half is the durable part. Long-horizon self-distillation drifts into hedging: on 50-100 tool-call trajectories the trained model's output concentrates on "wait," "but," and "maybe," which Malde attributes to per-step teachers each nudging toward reconsideration until reconsideration is the policy; the mitigation is to weight each step's distillation loss by the student/teacher KL divergence, a per-step weight rather than a KL penalty, so the signal decays once the rollout has left the region where the teacher's correction applies. And hint leakage is named as "the equivalent for OPSD" of reward hacking: a hint containing the resolution produces rollouts like "Oh, actually, I know what the solution is. It's 000. So, let me go back and put that into my reasoning trace," a behavior with no counterpart at inference; mitigations are an LLM hint-rewriter that supplies signals rather than resolutions ("what is something reasonable that they should have known") and residual guidance, blending a partial-hint teacher with the full-hint one to measure and clamp how far the hint pushes the policy out of its own distribution. The framing argument for the whole program is economic: benchmarks have "saturated within first years and then months," cost "4 hours, 6 hours, 24 hours or even several days" per task in some domains, and are "not tied to real world use cases," while "hundreds of trillions of tokens every single day" of inference already record how models fail. Q&A supplies the correction to the vision: the field is in "pseudo continual learning… batch updates offline and then re-uploading the model," with merging "10,000 rollouts going out in a product" into one update named as jointly an infrastructure and an algorithmic problem, and joint model/harness updating called "completely unexplored territory" — the second founder at this event to say so. Provenance: a founder talk for a company selling this pipeline, and **no measured number appears anywhere in it** — "87 out of 100" is an essay analogy, "65k" is an illustrative vocabulary size, both headline results are stated without values, both failure modes rest on a word cloud and a single rollout excerpt, hyperparameters are withheld for both mitigations, and the teacher forward passes that full-vocabulary matching requires are never priced against GRPO. Caption artifacts resolved: "WinSurf" → Windsurf, "sui 1" → SWE-1, "Fouian" → Faustian, "log props" → log probs, "buttweight" → "but wait", "waiting factor" → weighting factor, "wordle" → word cloud, "Daresh" → Dwarkesh, "Dakagon" → Decagon, "mercur apex" → Mercor APEX. Left unresolved: "Ilia Carpathi Schol" (a name-drop list) and "open claw"/"open claw rl"; the transcript also states both 12B and 120B for the scaled result, so no model size is cited for it anywhere in the wiki.
- `K0X9QDRkIdg` | [Anthropic's Applied AI team on the Evolution of Agentic Surfaces](../sources/20260811_K0X9QDRkIdg.md) | 2026-08-11 | transcript: `raw/20260811_K0X9QDRkIdg/K0X9QDRkIdg.en-orig.vtt` | Gagan Bhat and Isabella Kai He (Anthropic Applied AI) walk the surfaces an agent can be built on — Messages API ("simply tokens in and tokens out"), a hand-rolled loop ("painstaking"), the Claude Agent SDK ("we took the harness that powers Claude Code and we packaged it up"), and managed agents where Anthropic runs "everything below the product layer" — and turn the ladder into a decision by naming six things production forces on you regardless of rung: hosting and scaling, session management, filesystem, execution isolation, credentials, observability. The talk's organizing claim is about maintenance rather than architecture: "harnesses encode assumptions about what Claude cannot do on its own… they have to be questioned frequently because they go stale as models improve," summarized as "when the model moves and the harness doesn't, it degrades the agent." The worked instance is the most reusable thing in it because it has a named cost on both sides — Sonnet 4.5 "literally got anxious as it approached its context window limit" and wrapped up tasks early "even when it actually had room left to spare," the team added context-reset machinery, and on a later model that "no longer exhibited context anxiety" the same machinery "became dead weight. In fact, it became pure overhead, adding things like latency and causing issues with the cache being discarded incorrectly at times." The architecture half is a decoupling story told against their own first attempt: loop and container in one box, where "the container was blocking the agent being able to start its model reasoning" and "if one part of this component went down, the entire box of the agent would go down." Splitting the brain from the hands moved container start off the first-token path (a reported "60% faster time to first token for P50" and "over 90%" at P95), made a dead sandbox replaceable mid-run — "the brain could just spin up a new sandbox and retry and then continue as it left off" — and made a dead loop resumable from a session that is "a durable resource persisted in the cloud." That last piece carries the context-engineering contribution: "with many traditional harness implementations, the context window and the session are one in the same," so a mid-run discard loses the run, whereas a separate log means "the harness can actually just read in slices of that context from the session log into its current window." The object model is agent / environment / session with four session states (idle, running, rescheduling, terminated), the environment being a definition instantiated per session so policy like an egress allowlist is inherited. Security appears as two placements rather than policies: vaults that decrypt "only when needed at tool execution runtime… the model never sees your security tokens" — possible only because tool execution left the model's process — and MCP servers that "run only within their private network and only making outbound calls to the Claude agent loop," removing the inbound listener. A semi-interactive SRE demo (P99 latency "10x over baseline"; bash, grep, glob over uploaded logs plus MCP tools for deploys and metrics; an "SRE sandbox" with "allowed hosts only being the MCP server") shows the pieces together. Two frontier features are recorded with their gaps: **dreaming**, a periodic batch pass over session transcripts plus current memory that edits memory so "the next day's agent sessions [are] automatically much more intelligent," and **outcomes**, a user-defined rubric plus "a separate grader agent that runs alongside your agent loop" that "will keep trying until it reaches that success criteria that you have defined." The closing line is worth noting for who says it: a model lab arguing that "harnesses have become the limiting factor to what models can achieve." Provenance: an Anthropic vendor talk for its own managed-agent product, with no competitor comparison and no pricing anywhere. The two latency percentages are the only numbers in the talk and carry no workload, sample size, baseline description, or absolute values; context anxiety is anecdotal and the overhead it later caused is named but never quantified; dreaming and outcomes are announced with no evaluation, cost, iteration cap, conflict-resolution, or rollback; the demo is staged; and the security claims are architectural descriptions with no threat model for a compromised tool, a malicious skill, or exfiltration through an allowed host. Caption artifacts resolved: "Cloud" → Claude (pervasive), "Entropic" → Anthropic, "Gogin" → Gagan, "Manage Agents" → managed agents, "section management" → session management, "blob" → glob, "credential credentials" stutter. Left unresolved: "Claude Tag" (unidentifiable) and "Claude Opus 48" (4.5 vs 4.8 ambiguous in the captions), with no claim depending on either.
- `aeTb5BdmTTc` | [Agents, codebases, and teams — Aditya Khandelwal, Amazon AGI Lab](../sources/20260811_aeTb5BdmTTc.md) | 2026-08-11 | transcript: `raw/20260811_aeTb5BdmTTc/aeTb5BdmTTc.en-orig.vtt` | Aditya Khandelwal (Amazon AGI Lab), leading a team of about ten, argues that coding-agent adoption is a leadership problem rather than an IC one, on a structural rather than motivational ground: the two changes that move it — reorganizing the codebase for progressive disclosure and converging everyone onto a shared setup — are out of an IC's reach, since "if you want to change the way your code base is organized, you can't do that as an IC." The cost of leaving it to individuals is a self-reinforcing trap: engineers shipping ten PRs a day "look like… gods" beside those shipping one or two, and the one-to-two group inherits the review burden, so "not only can they not ship, they're going to actually see bad code and then curse the agents and hence not be able to get on to the let's ship 10 PRs" — a distributional sharpening of the wiki's aggregate review-capacity thread. The diagnostic layer is the most immediately usable part. Adoption state is plotted on fear ("is it coming for my job?" versus "a really handy tool") against actual utilization, with a non-monotonic observed trajectory: fear high and utilization low at the start, early adopters pulling utilization up, mandates and "token maxing" raising usage without confidence, and then shipped slop *lowering* fear for the wrong reason — "I'm not really that scared cuz… it just ships slop. Like I'm going to still be needed" — so a fear survey alone cannot separate confidence from resignation. Five symptoms say a setup is broken regardless of what anyone reports: engineers babysitting runs, long sessions needing constant intervention, "a constant slop factory," today's model "being really dumb" ("the model didn't change, right? The [harness] may have changed underneath"), and silent context burn where an uncomplicated task blows through several hundred thousand tokens into auto-compaction. Three principles follow — treat the codebase as the injection surface to "smartly prompt inject the model with just the right context at just the right time," with documentation living in the comments of the code a grep will hit so retrieval carries its own next hop; close the loop, because "slop is inevitable… you need to have a pipeline and a way to close the loop to remove the slop, to detect it, and to be able to like self-heal the system"; and keep iterating, since "you can't assume that you do this for a month and you're done," making shared-setup maintenance a standing fraction of IC time that "is not going to lead to like meaningful PRs like up front, but it's useful and it's worth it." The adoption wedge is one skill rather than a platform: their `ship it` carries a change from code-done to PR-ready (open the PR, write the description, handle review and merge comments, fix CI), "often… was running for over an hour," and the runtime is the proof rather than the problem — "I don't need to constantly baby sit it. I can trust it" — with skeptics' complaints routed back into the skill instead of answered. The failure list is the talk's most honest section: wiring agents into an issue tracker without wiring which agents may file produced 400–500 issues in a couple of weeks (the video description says roughly 4,500), "so many like different agents all trying to create issues cuz they've not been wired correctly"; long runtimes are an expectation problem, not a performance one; merge hell is accepted rather than solved; and prototype code is explicitly allowed to "opt out of all the rigorous other standards you've got across your code base" because it will never ship. The Q&A supplies the two most checkable artifacts anywhere in the talk: a hard limit of about 100 lines in a `SKILL.md` ("cuz your skill is really a folder"), an `AGENTS.md`/`CLAUDE.md` that is "a thin index that can point through the right files," and first-prompt context burn as the test of whether progressive disclosure is real — 20–25K tokens go regardless, and "if you're coming to like 40K, 50K, like something's wrong." Provenance: a ~17-minute practitioner talk about one team of about ten over a few months, with no before/after measurement of anything — PR counts are illustrative, the context thresholds are rules of thumb with no derivation, the nightly code gardener and agentic reviews are reported with no figure for what they caught, the `ship it` skill's contents are described but never shown, and the issue count conflicts with the description's "roughly 4,500." The "500k… 750k [or a] million" figures are cumulative session spend against auto-compaction, not a window size. Caption artifacts resolved: "hardness" → harness (pervasive), "Cloud MD" → `CLAUDE.md`, "agent's MD file" → `AGENTS.md`, "skill.md" → `SKILL.md`, "self-tours" → SEV-2s, "gigantic reviews" → agentic reviews (confirmed by the video description), "constant slot factory" → slop factory, "grapping" → grepping, "it trading on" → iterating on. Left unresolved: the 400–500 versus 4,500 issue-count discrepancy, recorded inline wherever the number is used, and "figure out your opinions" inside the `ship it` enumeration.
- `maRzp4kImJ4` | [Taking Reinforcement Learning Cross Datacenter — Nan Jiang, Modal](../sources/20260810_maRzp4kImJ4.md) | 2026-08-10 | transcript: `raw/20260810_maRzp4kImJ4/maRzp4kImJ4.en-orig.vtt` | Nan Jiang (Modal) argues that RL post-training is hard to buy capacity for because it demands four scarce properties at once — enough GPU, in the same region, on a fast fabric, available now — and that unlike inference, "training capacity is not elastic": a synchronous job's size is fixed at run start, so the industry's fragmented "bazaar" of supply cannot serve it. The talk's move is to cut the job on the absence of a global all-reduce rather than on "training versus inference": back-propagation stays colocated, while a rollout serving island — a coherent endpoint serving one policy version, permitted to keep intra-island collectives like tensor parallelism and prefill/decode disaggregation — is the unit that can leave. That leaves one link, the weight update, and a full frontier checkpoint at "roughly 500 gigabytes" takes "multiple minutes to hours" to move, which Jiang calls "the wrong unit of synchronization" when async training still wants weight-update latency "within seconds." The numerical argument that makes the split cheap is a floor-versus-push comparison over rollout-visible weights only (the served FP8/NVFP4 view, not FP32 master weights or Adam moments): BF16's ULP is about theta over 128 and the visibility boundary about half of that, while an Adam step is bounded at B x LR because Adam divides by running gradient statistics, giving a typical RL post-training step of about three millionths — over 1000x below the boundary. Because the floor scales with weight magnitude and the push does not, small weights move and large weights freeze; the reported consequence is that roughly 99% of served weights are bit-identical after a step, and the property survives staleness. Jiang stops on stage to block the obvious misreading: this is not gradient sparsity, since about 99% of parameters receive non-zero gradients and the FP32 master update is dense — LoRA is small by construction, a full-parameter delta is small by absorption. The sync object is therefore a bitwise-lossless patch of changed positions plus replacement bits, applied as selective overrides or XOR so that "it's not a floating point addition. So there's no additive delta drift," and lower serving precision makes it smaller still because a coarser format has a higher floor. The one first-party measurement is an internal GLM 4.7 Air run served in FP8: 0.15% of weights changed on the first high-learning-rate step, settling near 0.05%. Architecturally the trainer publishes immutable numbered rollout-weights versions to a bulletin board in Hugging Face safetensors layout — chosen "because it is accepted widely by many rollout engines such as SGLang and vLLM" — which engines outside the cluster pull without RDMA to the trainer; requests carry a target version and acceptable versions, responses carry the version actually served plus tokens, log probs and router-replay information; and a version-aware sidecar beside a stock server either proxies, applies the missing transitions, or answers "not ready." Modal's implementation is called Stitch, is framework-agnostic about trainer, engine and transport, and is pitched as "async first and also agentic first," with the payoff claim that "scattered inference capacity became one elastic rollout fleet" so that "inference capacity can now become RL capacity." Provenance: a vendor talk whose load-bearing evidence — the Adam bound, the ~99% bit-identical figure, the ~99% dense-gradient figure — is attributed to a paper the talk never names; the only first-party number is that one GLM run; no end-to-end result (no reward curve, wall-clock, or cost comparison against a colocated baseline) is shown anywhere; "extremely fast, in seconds" is derived from the 500 GB to 500 MB ratio rather than measured; the cost of constructing, storing and retaining per-step patches is never priced; the mechanism is learning-rate sensitive by its own arithmetic, which the 0.15%-versus-0.05% split illustrates; and Jiang himself names the scope limit — everything assumes Adam, while "Moonshot and DeepSeek" are adopting Muon, for which he does not know whether the sparsity holds. No trust or threat model is offered for running rollouts on third-party capacity, and there was no Q&A. Caption artifacts resolved include "ULP" spellings, "safe tensors" → safetensors, "NVFP4"/"MXFP4" format names, "Mion" → Muon, "SG Lang" → SGLang, and "serve version" → served version; four artifacts were left unresolved with no claim depending on any of them.
